\medskip
\begin{minipage}{0.95\textwidth}
\footnotesize
\textit{Note:} Entries report the number of active months in which each classification applies. Global and local-only bridge columns indicate incidence to at least one edge of the corresponding type. Classifications can coexist at a node, although global bridges are excluded from the local-only edge set. Examples illustrate contrasting patterns rather than a ranking.
\end{minipage}
\end{table}

Since a global bridge is an edge whose removal separates its endpoints into different connected components, incidence to such an edge can indicate a connection between groups of nodes, but it can also describe the attachment of a peripheral node to the rest of the network.

Dunkirk LNG Terminal provides a clear example of the latter case. In all its 56 active months, it has one neighbour in the undirected transport network and its sole incident edge is a global bridge. It is nevertheless never an articulation point: removing Dunkirk does not split the remaining network, whereas removing its incident edge isolates Dunkirk. Its classification therefore describes peripheral attachment rather than a role in connecting other nodes.

Conversely, a node can be an articulation point without being incident to a global bridge. The Strait of Hormuz is an articulation point in all 60 months but is incident to a global bridge in only 58. In the remaining two months, removing the node separates the network even though removing any individual incident edge does not. This distinction illustrates why node-removal and edge-removal indicators provide different information.

Local-only bridges identify another structural feature. Their endpoints have no common neighbour, but removing the edge does not disconnect the component because a longer alternative path remains. They therefore indicate the absence of an immediate two-edge alternative through a shared neighbour, rather than an edge that is indispensable to overall connectivity.

Nigeria LNG Terminal is incident to at least one local-only bridge in all its 59 active months, and Sabine Pass in 58 of 60. Among chokepoints, Malacca Strait has local-only bridge incidence in every month, while Suez Canal has it in 59 of 60 months (Table~\ref{tab:node_structural_examples}). 

These classifications can coexist with articulation status or global bridge incidence because a node may have several incident edges with different structural properties.
For instance, Malacca Strait is an articulation point and is incident to a global bridge in 54 months, while also being incident to a local-only bridge in all 60 months. 
This combination indicates that some connections have longer alternative paths, whereas other connections, or the node as a whole, maintain connectivity between otherwise separated parts of the network. 
It is to be noted that the alternative paths identified in the undirected projection are not necessarily feasible substitutes for the original LNG movements: these indicators do not account for direction, navigability or transport capacity.

These results distinguish the frequent intermediate connectivity roles of chokepoints from the more varied structural positions of terminals. 

\section{Associations and modelling implications}
\label{sec:associations_variable_selection}

Spearman rank correlations provide a preliminary assessment of the overlap among variables considered in the subsequent modelling analysis. 

Among the terminal covariates, lagged log-transformed throughput is strongly associated with lagged log-transformed voyage count (\(\rho_S=0.989\)), whereas its associations with other terminal characteristics are substantially weaker. 
Its correlation with lagged log-transformed mean voyage distance is 0.223 when the latter is defined, while the corresponding correlations with terminal capacity and terminal age are 0.423 and 0.199, respectively. 
Neighbour activity is also only weakly associated with these node-specific characteristics. 
These patterns suggest that, beyond the strong association between throughput and voyage count, the candidate covariates capture aspects of terminal activity that cannot be explained solely by operational scale.

A similar pattern is observed among the chokepoint covariates. 
Lagged log-transformed throughput is strongly associated with lagged log-transformed voyage count (\(\rho_S=0.992\)), whereas its associations with the lagged share of active neighbours (\(\rho_S=0.118\)) and lagged log-transformed mean voyage distance (\(\rho_S=-0.057\)) are substantially weaker. 
The seasonal terms also show little association with lagged throughput. 
Beyond the close correspondence between throughput and voyage count, the chokepoint covariates therefore capture distinct aspects of activity, route characteristics, and temporal variation.

The associations among the criticality measures differ between terminals and chokepoints. 
Among active terminal--month observations, terminal- and country-level counterparty HHIs are strongly correlated ($\rho_S=0.920$), reflecting their related but distinct measures of trade concentration. 
Their associations with role-specific PageRank are negative and more moderate in magnitude ($-0.596$ and $-0.545$, respectively), while role-specific dependence is more weakly associated with the other terminal measures. 

For active chokepoint--month observations, the three continuous measures show stronger positive associations. 
Flow share correlates with weighted betweenness at 0.810 and with PageRank at 0.704, while the correlation between PageRank and betweenness is 0.706. 
Thus, although the individual comparisons in Section~\ref{sec:chokepoint_network_position} show that greater flow exposure does not necessarily correspond to higher centrality, the aggregate associations indicate that chokepoints carrying larger shares of observed LNG flows tend to have higher PageRank and betweenness values. 
The correlations remain below one, consistent with the measures capturing related but distinct dimensions of chokepoint criticality.

Figure~\ref{fig:criticality_correlations} summarises the pairwise associations among the terminal and chokepoint criticality measures. 
Taken together, these patterns provide descriptive evidence of shared variation across multiple dimensions of node criticality while preserving meaningful differences between the individual measures. 
These findings provide the empirical motivation for investigating whether the measures' joint variation can be represented by a lower-dimensional structure in the modelling framework developed in Chapter~\ref{chap:criticality_modelling}.

% Chokepoint run: 20261011T154621463157Z_chokepoint_bounded.
\chapter{Bayesian Reduced-Rank model for network measures}
\label{chap:criticality_modelling}

This chapter develops a Bayesian reduced-rank
framework to investigate common patterns across
multiple measures of nodes' positions within the network and
their associations with node characteristics and
lagged network conditions.

Separate rank-one latent factor models are
estimated for terminals and chokepoints,
reflecting their different functions and response
measures within the LNG maritime network.
Each model includes its own set of response variables,
through a shared latent factor, modelled as a function of the corresponding set of covariates.

The analysis focuses on node-month observations
for which activity is observed in consecutive months, using a
static specification to examine associations
between network position and the selected
covariates. The resulting latent factors provide
node-specific summaries of network position. 
Their interpretation and adequacy are evaluated
through posterior inference, predictive checks
and a prior sensitivity analysis.


% TODO: Update introductory description after Models 2 and 3 are specified.
\section{General modelling framework}
% Shared estimation samples, responses, likelihoods, and baseline priors.
\subsection{Estimation samples, responses and covariates}
\label{sec:criticality_sample}
\label{sec:simple_responses}

The terminal model-ready panel contains 9,540 observations
for 159 terminals from January 2020 to December 2024.

For the current specification, an observation is retained
when the terminal is active in month $t$, a lag from the
preceding calendar month is available, and the terminal
was also active in that month. This restriction allows
the analysis to focus on network position among
consecutively active terminals, using information
from the preceding month without modelling transitions
between active and inactive states. 

After excluding 4,988 inactive observations,
71 active observations without an available calendar
lag, and 916 observations preceded by inactivity
the sample contains 3,565 observations active in consecutive
months. A further 112 observations with missing
terminal age are excluded, resulting in an estimation
sample of size 3,453 observations for 137 terminals.
Of these observations, 2,325 have lagged importer status and
1,128 have lagged exporter status.

The terminal model includes six responses:
three bounded measures (role-specific PageRank,
terminal counterparty HHI and role-specific dependence)
and three binary indicators (articulation-point status,
incident-global-bridge status and connection to an
articulation point). These measures are expected to capture different
aspects of network position and need not all increase
with the same latent dimension.

Seven predictors are included: lagged
historical-neighbour activity share, log-transformed
lagged mean voyage distance, log-transformed
lagged terminal capacity, monthly sine and cosine terms,
terminal age, and an indicator for exporter status
in the preceding month. Numeric covariates are
standardised, while the exporter indicator
remains on its zero--one scale. Accordingly,
coefficients for numeric predictors correspond
to one-standard-deviation differences, whereas
the role coefficient compares lagged exporters
with lagged importers, holding other covariates fixed.

The bounded responses are modelled on their original
scales. PageRank lies strictly within $(0,1)$,
whereas HHI and dependence take values in $(0,1]$.
Neither HHI nor dependence has observed zeros,
but HHI equals one in 34.09\% of observations
and dependence equals one in 20.76\%.
These features motivate a Beta likelihood for
PageRank and one-inflated Beta likelihoods for
HHI and dependence, as specified in
Section~\ref{subsec:shared_response_likelihoods}.

The chokepoint panel contains 1,440 observations
for 24 nodes. Applying the same calendar-lag
and consecutive-activity criteria excludes
200 currently inactive observations, 21 active
observations without an available calendar lag,
and 56 observations preceded by inactivity.
The resulting estimation sample contains
1,163 observations for all 24 chokepoints.

The chokepoint model includes five responses:
PageRank, betweenness, share of monthly network
flow, articulation-point status, and connection
to an articulation point. The incident-global-bridge
indicator is excluded because it differs from
articulation-point status in only three observations
and the correlation between the two binary indicators is 0.993.

Four predictors are included: lagged
historical-neighbour activity share, log-transformed
lagged mean voyage distance, and monthly sine and cosine
terms. All four predictors are standardised within the
chokepoint estimation sample.

PageRank and flow share lie strictly within
$(0,1)$, while betweenness has six exact zeros
(0.516\%) and otherwise takes values in $(0,1)$.
None of these responses equals one. Consequently,
PageRank and flow share are modelled using Beta
likelihoods, while betweenness uses a zero-inflated
Beta likelihood.


\subsection{Response likelihoods}
\label{subsec:shared_response_likelihoods}
The following response families are used for the baseline model and provide the likelihood framework for the proposed extensions. The expressions below use a scalar factor $f_{i,t}$ and its response-specific loading, as in Model~1; their latent linear predictors will require extension for the rank-two model.
For bounded response $j$, the interior mean and precision are defined as
\begin{align}
 \operatorname{logit}(\mu_{i,t,j})&=\alpha_j+\lambda_j f_{i,t},\\
 A_{i,t,j}&=\mu_{i,t,j}\phi_j,\qquad
 D_{i,t,j}=(1-\mu_{i,t,j})\phi_j.
\end{align}
For terminals, $j=1$ denotes PageRank, $j=2$ HHI, and $j=3$ dependence. For $j=1$, the PageRank follows
\begin{equation}
 Y_{i,t,1}\mid f_{i,t}\sim\operatorname{Beta}(A_{i,t,1},D_{i,t,1}).
\end{equation}
For HHI and dependence ($j=2,3$), the boundary probability has a separate loading:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{logit}(\pi_{i,t,j})
 &= c^{T}_j+d^{T}_j f_{i,t},
 \qquad j=2,3,\\
 Y_{i,t,j}\mid f_{i,t}
 &\sim \pi_{i,t,j}\delta_1
 +(1-\pi_{i,t,j})
 \operatorname{Beta}(A_{i,t,j},D_{i,t,j}).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:one_inflated_beta}
\end{equation}
where $\delta_1$ denotes a point mass at one. 
Interior observations contribute the Beta density multiplied by $1-\pi_{i,t,j}$, while exact ones contribute by the probability $\pi_{i,t,j}$. 
Observed values are not clipped or displaced from the boundary. The overall conditional mean is
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb{E}(Y_{i,t,j}\mid f_{i,t})=
 \pi_{i,t,j}+(1-\pi_{i,t,j})\mu_{i,t,j}.
 \label{eq:inflated_mean}
\end{equation}
Thus the sign of $\lambda_j$ alone does not determine how the overall mean changes with the factor.

For chokepoints, PageRank and flow share follow the interior Beta specification. 
For betweenness, let $\rho_{i,t,2}$ denote the probability of zero:
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{logit}(\rho_{i,t,2})
 &= c^{C}_2+d^{C}_2 f_{i,t},\\
 Y_{i,t,2}\mid f_{i,t}
 &\sim \rho_{i,t,2}\delta_0
 +(1-\rho_{i,t,2})
 \operatorname{Beta}(A_{i,t,2},D_{i,t,2}),
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:zero_inflated_beta}
\end{equation}
where $\delta_0$ denotes a point mass at zero. The overall conditional mean is $(1-\rho_{i,t,2})\mu_{i,t,2}$. 
The zero-probability loading is estimated separately from the interior-mean loading.

For binary response $k$,
\begin{align}
 B_{i,t,k}\mid f_{i,t}&\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p_{i,t,k}),\\
 \operatorname{logit}(p_{i,t,k})&=a_k+\ell_k f_{i,t}.
\end{align}

\subsection{Prior specification}
\label{subsec:prior_distributions}

The following priors specify the shared response parameters and
the rank-one baseline factor regression. Additional priors and
identification constraints required by Models~2 and~3 will be
stated with those extensions. The terminal and chokepoint models
are estimated separately, with independent parameters.

The prior distributions were chosen to provide regularisation
while allowing sufficient flexibility in the relationships
between the latent factor and the observed responses. Let $q$
denote the number of covariates, with $q=7$ for terminals and
$q=4$ for chokepoints. The factor-regression coefficients were
assigned independent zero-centred Normal priors,
$\beta_h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^2)$ for $h=1,\ldots,q$, favouring
moderate covariate associations on the scale of the latent
factor.

The loading associated with PageRank was constrained to be
positive to fix the orientation of the latent factor, while
the remaining loadings were allowed to take either sign.
For the three bounded responses, the loading priors were
$\lambda_1\sim\operatorname{HalfNormal}(1)$ and
$\lambda_j\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ for $j=2,3$.
The binary-response loadings were assigned independent priors
$\ell_k\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ for $k=1,\ldots,K$, where $K=3$
for terminals and $K=2$ for chokepoints.

For the interior components of the terminal bounded responses,
intercept priors were specified on the logit scale, with centres
chosen to reflect differences in the empirical scales of the
corresponding network measures. In particular, PageRank
typically takes values close to zero, making a zero-centred
intercept prior, corresponding to an inverse-logit value of
0.5, which is inappropriate. Its intercept prior was therefore centred
at $-4$, corresponding to an inverse-logit value of approximately
0.018. The intercept priors for HHI and dependence were instead
centred at zero, allowing their interior means to vary around
a reference value of 0.5 when the factor is zero. These choices
correspond to $\alpha_j\sim\mathcal{N}(m_j,1.5^2)$ for
$j=1,2,3$, with $(m_1,m_2,m_3)=(-4,0,0)$.

Binary-response intercepts were assigned independent
$a_k\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2^2)$ priors, allowing a relatively broad
range of probabilities where the latent factor equals zero. For terminal HHI
and dependence, the intercepts governing the probability of
an exact one were assigned
$c_j^T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1.5^2)$ priors, while their separate
factor loadings followed $d_j^T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$,
for $j=2,3$.

The precision parameters of the bounded continuous components
were assigned independent log-Normal priors, with
$\log\phi_j\sim\mathcal{N}(\log 10,0.7^2)$ for $j=1,2,3$.
This specification ensures positive precision values, with
prior median 10 and an approximate central 95\% interval of
$[2.54,39.44]$, allowing different degrees of dispersion around
the conditional interior means.

For chokepoints, the same prior families and scales were
adopted, with parameters estimated independently from the
terminal model. The interior intercept centres were set to
$(m_1,m_2,m_3)=(-4,-5,-4)$ for PageRank, betweenness, and flow
share, respectively, reflecting the relatively small values
typically observed for these measures. In particular, the
inverse-logit of $-5$ is approximately 0.0067, comparable to
the empirical median of chokepoint betweenness.

The betweenness zero-component intercept was assigned
$c_2^C\sim\mathcal{N}(-5,1.5^2)$, expressing a prior preference
for a low probability of exact zeros at factor value zero.
Its separate factor loading followed
$d_2^C\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$. Given the small number of observed
zeros, inference on this component may be particularly
sensitive to the prior specification.

All response-parameter priors were specified independently
of one another and of the factor-regression coefficients.
These distributions were adopted as initial regularising
priors rather than independently elicited prior information.
Their prior predictive implications and the sensitivity of
the posterior findings to alternative specifications are
examined in Section~\ref{sec:prior_sensitivity}.

\section{Model specifications} 
\label{sec:models}

\subsection{Baseline rank-one specification}
\label{subsec:simple_factor_specification}
For each node type separately, let $\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}$ be the standardised covariate vector and $f_{i,t}$ be a scalar latent factor. The factor regression is
\begin{equation}
 f_{i,t}\mid\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t},\boldsymbol{\beta}
 \sim\mathcal{N}(\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta},1).
 \label{eq:simple_factor}
\end{equation}
The residual factor variance is fixed at one to define its scale. 
The PageRank loading is constrained to be positive to orient the factor, while the other loadings are unrestricted. 
Conditional on the factors and parameters, the responses are assumed independent, and factors for different node-months are independent given their covariates and regression coefficients.

Let $\boldsymbol{\gamma}$ denote the vector of loadings for all
response components:
\begin{equation*}
\boldsymbol{\gamma}
=
\begin{cases}
(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3,
 \ell_1,\ell_2,\ell_3,d_2^T,d_3^T)^{\mathsf T},
& \text{for terminals},\\
(\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3,
 \ell_1,\ell_2,d_2^C)^{\mathsf T},
& \text{for chokepoints}.
\end{cases}
\end{equation*}
For component $m=1,\ldots,M$, let $\eta_{i,t,m}$ denote
the linear predictor on the logit scale: the logit of
the interior Beta mean, the binary-response probability,
or the boundary probability, according to the component.
It can be written as
$\eta_{i,t,m}=\xi_m+\gamma_m f_{i,t}$,
where $\xi_m$ and $\gamma_m$ denote the corresponding
intercept and loading, respectively, with $M=8$ for
terminals and $M=6$ for chokepoints.

Substituting
$f_{i,t}=\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}+z_{i,t}$,
where $z_{i,t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ independently across
node--month observations, gives
\begin{equation}
\eta_{i,t,m}
=
\xi_m+
\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}^{\mathsf T}
(\gamma_m\boldsymbol{\beta})
+\gamma_m z_{i,t}.
\end{equation}
Thus, the covariate coefficient vector for component
$m$ is $\gamma_m\boldsymbol{\beta}$.
Stacking these vectors as rows gives
\begin{equation*}
\begin{pmatrix}
\gamma_1\beta_1 & \cdots & \gamma_1\beta_q\\
\vdots & \ddots & \vdots\\
\gamma_M\beta_1 & \cdots & \gamma_M\beta_q
\end{pmatrix}
=
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T},
\end{equation*}
where $q$ denotes the number of covariates.
Every row is a scalar multiple of
$\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}$, so the matrix has
rank at most one.
This structure provides a parsimonious summary of how
the covariates are associated with multiple network
measures through a shared latent dimension, while
allowing each response component to relate to that
dimension with a different strength and sign.

Inference for this model is based on the joint posterior
distribution of both the parameters and latent factors,
which describes their uncertainty conditional on the
observed responses and covariates. By using the Bayes' theorem,
this distribution is proportional to the product of
the response likelihood, the conditional distribution
of the latent factors given the covariates and regression
coefficients, and the parameter prior densities
\citep{gelman2013bayesian}.
These components are specified below, followed by the
joint posterior distribution and the full conditional
posterior kernels. Detailed derivations are provided in
Appendix~\ref{app:baseline_full_conditionals}.

Let $\mathcal{O}$ denote the set of node--month observations
included in estimation. To simplify the notations, each
observation $(i,t)\in\mathcal{O}$ is indexed by $n$, so that
$f_n$, $\boldsymbol{x}_n$, $Y_{nj}$, and $B_{nk}$ correspond
to $f_{i,t}$, $\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}$, $Y_{i,t,j}$, and
$B_{i,t,k}$, respectively. 

Let $\mathbf{f}=(f_n)_{n\in\mathcal{O}}$ collect the latent
factors. The observed responses are $Y_{nj}$ for the three
bounded measures, $j=1,2,3$, and $B_{nk}$ for the binary
measures, $k=1,\ldots,K$, where $K=3$ for terminals and
$K=2$ for chokepoints.

Let $\boldsymbol{\theta}$ collect the response-specific
parameters: the bounded-response intercepts $\alpha_j$,
loadings $\lambda_j$, and precisions $\phi_j$; the
binary-response intercepts $a_k$ and loadings $\ell_k$;
and the boundary-component parameters $c_j$ and $d_j$.
The latter denote $c_j^T,d_j^T$ for terminal HHI and
dependence, $j=2,3$, or $c_2^C,d_2^C$ for chokepoint
betweenness.

Under the conditional-independence assumption stated above,
the joint likelihood contribution for a single
node--month is the product of the individual response likelihood, 
denoted by $g_j(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})$,
and the Bernoulli probabilities of the binary responses:
\begin{equation*}
p(\mathbf{Y}_n,\mathbf{B}_n
  \mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})=
\prod_{j=1}^{3}
g_j(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})
\prod_{k=1}^{K}
p_{nk}^{B_{nk}}
(1-p_{nk})^{1-B_{nk}}.
\end{equation*}
Combining the response contributions over all observations
in $\mathcal{O}$ gives the likelihood
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
L(\boldsymbol{\theta},\mathbf{f})
&=
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(\mathbf{Y}_n,\mathbf{B}_n
  \mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})
\\
&=
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\left[
\prod_{j=1}^{3}
g_j(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})
\prod_{k=1}^{K}
p_{nk}^{B_{nk}}
(1-p_{nk})^{1-B_{nk}}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:baseline_response_likelihood}
\end{equation}

The factor regression in Equation~(\ref{eq:simple_factor})
gives the joint conditional density of the latent factors:
\begin{equation}
p(\mathbf{f}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{X})
=
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\mathcal{N}\!\left(
f_n;
\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta},1
\right),
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{X}$ is the covariate design matrix,
with row $\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}$ for observation $n$.

Combining these components and denoting the observed
responses and covariates by $\mathcal{D}$, the joint
posterior density is therefore proportional to
\begin{equation}
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{f}
\mid\mathcal{D})
\propto
L(\boldsymbol{\theta},\mathbf{f})
\,p(\mathbf{f}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{X})
\,p(\boldsymbol{\theta})p(\boldsymbol{\beta}).
\label{eq:joint_posterior}
\end{equation}
where $p(\boldsymbol{\theta})$ and
$p(\boldsymbol{\beta})$ are the products of the independent
prior densities introduced in
Section~\ref{subsec:prior_distributions}.

The full conditional posterior kernels are obtained by retaining
only the terms in Equation~(\ref{eq:joint_posterior}) that depend
on the parameter of interest, with all remaining
unknown quantities held fixed. In the expressions below,
$\textit{rest}$ denotes these remaining quantities together
with the observed data and covariates.

Define
$\mathcal{I}_j=\{n\in\mathcal{O}:0<Y_{nj}<1\}$, so that 
for ordinary Beta responses, $\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{O}$, while
for boundary-inflated responses it contains only the
interior observations.

\paragraph{Binary-response loadings.}

For each binary-response loading $k=1,\ldots,K$, the
conditional posterior distribution is
\begin{equation}
p(\ell_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{\exp\{B_{nk}(a_k+\ell_k f_n)\}}
{1+\exp(a_k+\ell_k f_n)}
\right]
\exp\left(-\frac{\ell_k^2}{2}\right).
\label{eq:fc_binary_loading}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Bounded-response interior loadings.}

For the unrestricted interior-mean loadings
$\lambda_j$, $j=2,3$, corresponding to terminal HHI
and dependence or chokepoint betweenness and flow share,
the full conditional kernels are
\begin{equation}
p(\lambda_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda_j^2}{2}\right),
\qquad j=2,3,
\label{eq:fc_beta_loading}
\end{equation}
For PageRank, the half-Normal prior restricts the
loading to positive values:
\begin{equation}
p(\lambda_1\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
Y_{n1}^{\mu_{n1}\phi_1-1}
(1-Y_{n1})^{(1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n1}\phi_1)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1)
}
\right]
\exp\left(-\frac{\lambda_1^2}{2}\right)
\mathbb{I}(\lambda_1>0).
\label{eq:fc_pagerank_loading}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Boundary-probability loadings.}

For terminal HHI and dependence, define
$Z_{nj}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{nj}=1\}}$, $j=2,3$.
The full conditional kernels are
\begin{equation}
p(d_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^T f_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^T f_n)
}
\right]
\exp\left[-\frac{(d_j^T)^2}{2}\right],
\qquad j=2,3.
\label{eq:fc_one_loading}
\end{equation}
For chokepoint betweenness, let
$Z_{n2}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{n2}=0\}}$
The full conditional kernel is
\begin{equation}
p(d_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{n2}(c_2^C+d_2^C f_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^C f_n)
}
\right]
\exp\left[-\frac{(d_2^C)^2}{2}\right].
\label{eq:fc_zero_loading}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Factor-regression coefficients.}

The Gaussian factor regression and the independent
$\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^2)$ coefficient priors give the
full conditional kernel
\begin{equation}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}+4\mathbf{I}_q)
\boldsymbol{\beta}-
2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}
\Big]\Bigg\}.
\label{eq:fc_beta_kernel}
\end{equation}
Recognising the kernel of a multivariate Normal
distribution gives the conjugate full conditional
\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim\mathcal{N}_q\Big(
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f},
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\Big).
\label{eq:fc_beta_normal}
\end{equation}
Matrix notation is used here for compactness, with
$\mathbf{I}_q$ denoting the $q\times q$ identity matrix.

\paragraph{Latent factors.}

For each node--month observation, the full conditional
of the factor combines the Gaussian regression density
with the likelihood contributions of all observed responses:
\begin{equation}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
L_n(f_n)
\exp\left[
-\frac12
(f_n-\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta})^2
\right].
\label{eq:fc_factor}
\end{equation}

For terminals, the observation-specific likelihood is
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
L_n^T(f_n)
={}&
p(Y_{n1}\mid f_n,\alpha_1,\lambda_1,\phi_1)
\\
&\times
\prod_{j=2}^{3}
p(Y_{nj}\mid
f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,c_j^T,d_j^T)
\\
&\times
\prod_{k=1}^{3}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:terminal_factor_likelihood}
\end{equation*}

For chokepoints, it is
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
L_n^C(f_n)
={}&
p(Y_{n1}\mid f_n,\alpha_1,\lambda_1,\phi_1)
\\
&\times
p(Y_{n2}\mid
f_n,\alpha_2,\lambda_2,\phi_2,c_2^C,d_2^C)
\\
&\times
p(Y_{n3}\mid f_n,\alpha_3,\lambda_3,\phi_3)
\\
&\times
\prod_{k=1}^{2}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:chokepoint_factor_likelihood}
\end{equation*}
The inflated-response terms in these expressions denote
the complete mixture contributions, including the
boundary probabilities and, for interior observations,
the Beta densities.

\paragraph{Binary-response intercepts.}

Under the prior $a_k\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2^2)$, the posterior is
\begin{equation}
p(a_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{a_k^2}{8}
+a_k\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}B_{nk}-
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\log\left[1+\exp(a_k+\ell_k f_n)\right]
\Bigg\}.
\label{eq:fc_binary_intercept_log}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Bounded-response interior intercepts.}

For $\alpha_j\sim\mathcal{N}(m_j,1.5^2)$,
the full conditional kernels are
\begin{equation}
p(\alpha_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\exp\left[
-\frac{(\alpha_j-m_j)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right],
\qquad j=1,2,3.
\label{eq:fc_beta_intercept}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Boundary-probability intercepts.}

For terminal HHI and dependence,
$c_j^T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1.5^2)$ gives
\begin{equation}
p(c_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^T f_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^T f_n)
}
\right]
\exp\left[
-\frac{(c_j^T)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right],
\qquad j=2,3.
\label{eq:fc_one_intercept}
\end{equation}
For chokepoint betweenness,
$c_2^C\sim\mathcal{N}(-5,1.5^2)$ gives
\begin{equation}
p(c_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{(c_2^C+5)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
+c_2^C\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}Z_{n2}-
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\log\left[1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^C f_n)\right]
\Bigg\}.
\label{eq:fc_zero_intercept}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Beta precision parameters.}

Under the prior assumption $\log\phi_j\sim\mathcal{N}(\log10,0.7^2)$,
the full conditional posterior for each bounded-response component $j=1,2,3$ is
\begin{equation}
p(\phi_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\frac{1}{\phi_j}
\exp\left[
-\frac{(\log\phi_j-\log10)^2}{2(0.7)^2}
\right],
\label{eq:fc_precision}
\end{equation}
with $\phi_j>0$.

Except for the Normal full conditional distribution in
Equation~(\ref{eq:fc_beta_normal}), the full conditionals
above do not generally have standard conjugate forms.
The joint posterior density is known up to a normalising
constant, but the integrals required for normalisation
and marginalisation are analytically intractable.
Inference is therefore based on numerical sampling
using the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS), whose implementation
and associated diagnostic criteria are described in
Section~\ref{sec:posterior_computation}.

\subsection{Random-intercept rank-one specification}
\label{subsec:node_specific_model}

The baseline rank-one specification assumes that the
conditional mean of the latent factor is entirely determined
by the observed covariates. However, nodes may exhibit
persistent differences in their network characteristics
that are not fully captured by these predictors.
To account for such unobserved heterogeneity, the model
is extended by introducing a node-specific random intercept
into the latent-factor regression.

Let $i=1,\ldots,N$ index the distinct nodes included
in the estimation sample. The extended factor regression is

\begin{equation}
f_{i,t}\mid
\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t},\boldsymbol{\beta},\mu_i
\sim
\mathcal{N}
\left(
\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
+\mu_i,1
\right),
\label{eq:node_specific_factor}
\end{equation}
where $\mu_i$ represents a persistent node-specific
deviation from the factor mean predicted by the observed
covariates. Equivalently,

\begin{equation}
f_{i,t}
=
\boldsymbol{x}_{i,t}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
+\mu_i+z_{i,t},
\qquad
z_{i,t}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1).
\label{eq:node_specific_decomposition}
\end{equation}
The effect $\mu_i$ is shared across all observations
belonging to node $i$, while $z_{i,t}$ represents
observation-specific residual variation.
The residual variance remains fixed at one, as in
the baseline specification.

The node-specific intercepts are assigned a hierarchical
Gaussian prior,

\begin{equation}
\mu_i\mid\sigma_\mu
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_\mu^2),
\qquad i=1,\ldots,N,
\label{eq:node_effect_prior}
\end{equation}
with

\begin{equation}
\sigma_\mu\sim\operatorname{HalfNormal}(1).
\label{eq:node_effect_scale_prior}
\end{equation}
The parameter $\sigma_\mu$ controls the magnitude
of persistent between-node heterogeneity:
small values imply stronger shrinkage of the
node-specific effects towards zero, approaching
the baseline specification, whereas larger values
allow greater differences across nodes.

The response-specific likelihoods, prior distributions
of the baseline parameters, and positive PageRank
loading constraint remain unchanged.
The extension therefore preserves the rank-one
structure of the covariate coefficient matrix
$\boldsymbol{\gamma}\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}$,
while introducing persistent node-specific
contributions $\gamma_m\mu_i$ to each response
component's linear predictor.

Using the observation index $n\in\mathcal{O}$
introduced in the baseline specification, let
$i(n)$ denote the node associated with observation $n$.
Define $\boldsymbol{\mu}=(\mu_1,\ldots,\mu_N)^{\mathsf T}$.
The conditional density of the latent factors is

\begin{equation}
p(\mathbf{f}\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{X})
=
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\mathcal{N}\left(
f_n;
\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
+\mu_{i(n)},1
\right).
\label{eq:node_factor_density}
\end{equation}
Combining this density with the response likelihood
and the hierarchical prior gives the joint posterior

\begin{equation}
p(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\beta},
\mathbf{f},\boldsymbol{\mu},\sigma_\mu
\mid\mathcal{D})
\propto
L(\boldsymbol{\theta},\mathbf{f})
\,p(\mathbf{f}\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{X})
p(\boldsymbol{\theta})
p(\boldsymbol{\beta})
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\sigma_\mu)
p(\sigma_\mu),
\label{eq:node_joint_posterior}
\end{equation}
where $L(\boldsymbol{\theta},\mathbf{f})$
is the response likelihood defined in
Equation~(\ref{eq:baseline_response_likelihood}),
$p(\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\sigma_\mu)
=\prod_{i=1}^{N}p(\mu_i\mid\sigma_\mu)$,
and $p(\sigma_\mu)$ is the Half-Normal prior
in Equation~(\ref{eq:node_effect_scale_prior}).

The full conditional posterior kernels follow
from Equation~(\ref{eq:node_joint_posterior}).
Since the response likelihood and its parameters
are unchanged, their full conditional kernels
retain the expressions derived for the baseline
model, conditional on the latent factors.
Only the conditional distributions involving
the factor regression and the newly introduced
hierarchical parameters require modification.

\paragraph{Factor-regression coefficients.}

Define the $|\mathcal{O}|$-dimensional vector
$\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}$ whose $n^{th}$ element
is $\mu_{i(n)}$.
The Gaussian factor regression and the independent
$\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^2)$ coefficient priors give

\begin{equation}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}+4\mathbf{I}_q)
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})
\Big]\Bigg\}.
\label{eq:node_fc_beta_kernel}
\end{equation}
Recognising the multivariate Normal kernel gives

\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim\mathcal{N}_q\Big(
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}),
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\Big).
\label{eq:node_fc_beta_normal}
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Latent factors.}

For each node--month observation, the full conditional
kernel combines the unchanged response likelihood
with the modified Gaussian factor regression:

\begin{equation}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
L_n(f_n)
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}
-\mu_{i(n)}
\right)^2
\right].
\label{eq:node_fc_factor}
\end{equation}
Here $L_n(f_n)$ denotes the same observation-specific
likelihood defined for terminals and chokepoints
in the baseline specification.

\paragraph{Node-specific intercepts.}

For each node $i$, let
$\mathcal{O}_i=\{n\in\mathcal{O}:i(n)=i\}$
denote its observations in the estimation sample,
and let $n_i=|\mathcal{O}_i|$.
Combining the Gaussian prior for $\mu_i$
with the factor regression gives

\begin{equation}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\exp\left(
-\frac{\mu_i^2}{2\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
\left(
f_n-\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mu_i
\right)^2
\right].
\label{eq:node_fc_mu_kernel}
\end{equation}
Completing the square yields the conjugate
Normal full conditional

\begin{equation}
\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim\mathcal{N}(m_{\mu_i},V_{\mu_i}),
\label{eq:node_fc_mu_normal}
\end{equation}
where

\begin{equation*}
V_{\mu_i}=
\left(
n_i+\frac{1}{\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)^{-1},
\qquad
m_{\mu_i}=
V_{\mu_i}
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
\left(
f_n-\boldsymbol{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}
\right).
\end{equation*}

\paragraph{Node-effect scale parameter.}

The full conditional kernel for $\sigma_\mu$
combines its Half-Normal prior with the
Gaussian hierarchical distribution of the
node-specific intercepts:

\begin{equation}
p(\sigma_\mu\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\mathbb{I}(\sigma_\mu>0)
\,\sigma_\mu^{-N}
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
\sigma_\mu^2+
\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mu_i^2}
{\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)
\right].
\label{eq:node_fc_sigma_kernel}
\end{equation}
Unlike the conditional distribution of the
node-specific intercepts, this kernel does
not correspond to a standard conjugate
distribution for $\sigma_\mu$.

For posterior computation, the hierarchical
model is implemented through an equivalent
non-centred parametrisation,

\begin{equation}
u_i\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1),
\qquad
\mu_i=\sigma_\mu u_i.
\label{eq:node_effect_noncentered}
\end{equation}
This transformation preserves the prior
$\mu_i\mid\sigma_\mu\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_\mu^2)$
and therefore leaves the posterior distribution
of the model parameters and latent factors unchanged.
It only changes the coordinates used for sampling,
with the aim of improving computational efficiency \citep{papaspiliopoulos2007general}.

\subsection{Rank-two specification}
\label{subsec_rank_two_model}
The rank-two specification extends the baseline model by introducing a second latent factor, allowing the dependence structure among the observed responses to be represented in a two-dimensional latent space. This extension is motivated by the limitations of the rank-one specification, particularly its restricted ability to reproduce certain patterns of association among network criticality indicators.

For each node \(i\) and month \(t\), the scalar latent factor is replaced by a two-dimensional vector

\[
\mathbf f_{i,t}
=
\begin{pmatrix}
f_{i,t,1}\\
f_{i,t,2}
\end{pmatrix}.
\]

The conditional distribution of the latent factors is specified as

\begin{equation}
\mathbf f_{i,t}\mid\mathbf x_{i,t},\mathbf B
\sim
\mathcal N_2
\left(
\mathbf B^\top\mathbf x_{i,t},
\mathbf I_2
\right),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathbf B\in\mathbb R^{q\times 2}\) is the matrix of factor-regression coefficients and \(\mathbf I_2\) denotes the two-dimensional identity matrix. Each element of \(\mathbf B\) is assigned the same Gaussian prior used for the regression coefficients in the baseline model,
$
B_{kh}\sim\mathcal N(0,0.5^2), h=1,2.
$
The two latent dimensions therefore have independent unit-variance Gaussian residuals, conditional on the observed covariates.

The observation models retain the likelihood families introduced in Section~\ref{subsec:simple_factor_specification}. 
The main modification concerns the linear predictors, in which the scalar product between a response-specific loading 
and the latent factor is replaced by the inner product between two-dimensional vectors.

For each continuous response \(j\), the conditional mean of the interior Beta component is given by

\[
\operatorname{logit}(\mu_{i,t,j})
=
\alpha_j+
\boldsymbol\lambda_j^\top\mathbf f_{i,t},
\]
where \(\boldsymbol\lambda_j\in\mathbb R^2\) contains the two factor loadings associated with response \(j\).
Similarly, for each binary response \(k\),

\[
\operatorname{logit}(p_{i,t,k})
=
a_k+
\boldsymbol\ell_k^\top\mathbf f_{i,t},
\]
where \(\boldsymbol\ell_k\in\mathbb R^2\) denotes the corresponding loading vector.

The probabilities associated with the boundary components of the inflated Beta distributions are extended analogously, 
with a separate two-dimensional loading vector for each boundary response.

The prior distributions of the intercepts and precision parameters remain unchanged, while the loading priors 
are extended independently to the two latent dimensions. As in the baseline model, the first PageRank loading is 
constrained to be positive through a half-normal prior, $\lambda_{\mathrm{PR},1}\sim\operatorname{halfNormal}(1)$,
whereas the second PageRank loading follows an unconstrained Gaussian prior $\lambda_{\mathrm{PR},2}\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$.
All remaining loadings follow the corresponding Gaussian priors specified for the rank-one model.

The joint distribution of the rank-two model retains the conditional-independence structure of the baseline rank-one specification. 
Let \(\mathbf F=\{\mathbf f_n\}_{n=1}^{N}\) denote the collection of two-dimensional latent factors, 
and let \(\boldsymbol\theta\) contain the factor-regression coefficients, response-specific loadings, intercepts, and precision parameters. 
The joint distribution can be expressed as

\begin{equation}
p(\mathbf Y,\mathbf F,\boldsymbol\theta\mid\mathbf X)=
p(\boldsymbol\theta)
\prod_{n=1}^{N}
\left[
\mathcal N_2
\left(
\mathbf f_n;
\mathbf B^\top\mathbf x_n,
\mathbf I_2
\right)
\prod_{j=1}^{J}
p(Y_{nj}\mid\mathbf f_n,\boldsymbol\theta)
\right].
\end{equation}
Here, \(p(\boldsymbol\theta)\) represents the joint prior distribution of the model parameters, while the response likelihoods retain the distributions defined for the corresponding terminal and chokepoint models in Section~\ref{subsec:simple_factor_specification}.

The rank-two specification is estimated separately for terminals and chokepoints, using the same response variables, covariates, and estimation samples as the corresponding rank-one models. 
No node-specific random intercepts are included in this extension, allowing the effect of increasing the latent rank to be examined separately from the introduction of persistent node heterogeneity.

\section{Posterior computation}
\label{sec:posterior_computation}

\subsection{NUTS sampling algorithm and implementation}
\label{subsec:posterior_sampling}

The models were implemented in Python 3.12.15 using
PyMC 5.20.1 \citep{abrilpla2023pymc}, with PyTensor 2.27.1
as the computational backend. Posterior inference was
performed using the No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS)
\citep{hoffman2014no}, an adaptive variant of Hamiltonian
Monte Carlo (HMC) \citep{neal2011mcmc}. Unlike traditional
random-walk MCMC methods, HMC exploits gradient
information to construct proposals that can explore
high-dimensional posterior distributions more efficiently.
NUTS extends HMC by automatically determining the
trajectory length, avoiding the need to manually specify
the number of integration steps.
The principles underlying
HMC and its NUTS extension are outlined below.

Let $\boldsymbol{\omega}
=(\boldsymbol{\theta},\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{f})$
be the vector containing all unknown response parameters, regression
coefficients, and latent factors. 
The sampler operates on an unconstrained
representation of these parameters $\mathbf{q}=T(\boldsymbol{\omega})$.
By the change-of-variables formula, the transformed
log-posterior density is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q})
=
\log p_{\boldsymbol{\omega}}\!\left(
T^{-1}(\mathbf{q})\mid\mathcal{D}
\right)
+
\log\left|
\det\frac{\partial T^{-1}(\mathbf{q})}
{\partial\mathbf{q}}
\right|.
\end{equation}

Following \citet{neal2011mcmc}, an auxiliary momentum
vector $\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{M})$
is introduced, where $\mathbf{M}$ is a positive-definite
mass matrix. Intuitively, HMC treats the current parameter values
$\mathbf{q}$ as the position of a moving particle.
The negative transformed
log-posterior defines a potential-energy landscape,
with lower values in regions of higher transformed
posterior density. The momentum allows the particle
to move through this landscape, while the gradient
of $\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q})$ guides its trajectory.

The negative log-density of the Gaussian momentum,
up to an additive constant, defines the kinetic
energy $\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{v}$.
Together with the potential energy
$-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q})$, this defines the
Hamiltonian:
\begin{equation}
H(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{v})
=
-\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q})
+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{v}.
\end{equation}
The corresponding equations of motion are
\begin{equation*}
\frac{d\mathbf{q}}{d\tau}
=
\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}H(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{v})
=
\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{v},
\qquad
\frac{d\mathbf{v}}{d\tau}
=
-\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}H(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{v})
=
\nabla_{\mathbf{q}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q}),
\end{equation*}
with $\tau$ denoting an artificial integration time.

These equations are approximated numerically using
leapfrog integration: for a step size $\epsilon$,
each integration step comprises the updates
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
 \mathbf{v}_{1/2}
 &=
 \mathbf{v}
 +\frac{\epsilon}{2}
 \nabla_{\mathbf{q}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q}),\\
 \mathbf{q}'
 &=
 \mathbf{q}
 +\epsilon\mathbf{M}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{1/2},\\
 \mathbf{v}'
 &=
 \mathbf{v}_{1/2}
 +\frac{\epsilon}{2}
 \nabla_{\mathbf{q}}\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{q}').
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:leapfrog_updates}
\end{equation}
These updates act on the complete state vector,
so regression coefficients, response parameters,
and latent factors are explored jointly rather
than through sequential parameter-specific
full conditional updates.

NUTS constructs trajectories by recursively expanding
a tree of leapfrog states in either the forward or backward
direction until the no-U-turn
criterion detects that the trajectory is doubling
back, a numerical stopping criterion is met,
or the maximum tree depth is reached.
A state from the constructed trajectory is then
selected according to a rule that preserves the
target distribution \citep{hoffman2014no}.

Algorithm~\ref{alg:baseline_sampling} summarises
the sampling procedure for the rank-one baseline model.

\begin{algorithm}[H]
\caption{NUTS sampling for rank-one baseline model}
\label{alg:baseline_sampling}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require Data $\mathcal{D}$; chains $C$;
warm-up iterations $W$; retained draws $S$;
target acceptance $\delta$.

\For{each chain $c=1,\ldots,C$}
    \State Initialise the unconstrained parameter vector $\mathbf{q}$.
    \State Initialise step size $\epsilon$ and diagonal mass matrix $\mathbf{M}$.
    \For{$m=1,\ldots,W+S$}
        \State Sample momentum $\mathbf{v}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{0},\mathbf{M})$.
        \State  Initialise the trajectory at $(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{v})$.
        \Repeat
            \State Sample a direction $d\sim\operatorname{Uniform}(\{-1,+1\})$.
            \State Extend the tree in direction $d$ by doubling the number of leapfrog steps.
            \State Update the candidate state according to the NUTS selection rule.
        \Until{a stopping criterion is met}
        \State Set $\mathbf{q}$ to the selected candidate position.
        \If{$m\leq W$}
            \State Adapt $\epsilon$ to approach target acceptance $\delta$.
            \State Adapt $\mathbf{M}$ to account for differences in parameter scales.
        \Else
            \State Store the joint posterior draw $\boldsymbol{\omega}^{(c,m-W)} =T^{-1}(\mathbf{q})$.
            \State Compute factor means $\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}$ and residuals
            $f_n-\mathbf{x}_{n}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}$ for all $n\in\mathcal{O}$.
        \EndIf
    \EndFor
\EndFor
\State \Return $S$ posterior draws per chain.
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

Each node-type model was estimated using four parallel chains, with 20,000 burn-in
iterations and 30,000 retained draws per chain.
The use of multiple chains allows convergence to be assessed
by comparing within-chain and between-chain variability
\citep{gelman2013bayesian}, while the number of iterations
was increased to allow more extensive sampler adaptation
during warm-up and improve the Monte Carlo precision of
posterior summaries through a larger number of retained draws.

The target acceptance probability was set to 0.80,
corresponding to the target acceptance rate used during
step-size adaptation in the warm-up phase
\citep{hoffman2014no}. The random seed was set to 20261008.

\subsection{Sampling diagnostics}
\label{subsec:sampling_diagnostics}

Posterior summaries and convergence diagnostics were
computed using ArviZ 0.21.0, with NumPy 1.26.4,
pandas 2.3.3, and Matplotlib 3.9.2 used for data
processing and visualisation.

The assessment covered regression coefficients,
response parameters, latent factors, factor means,
and factor residuals. Several complementary diagnostics
were considered together to examine agreement across
chains, sampling efficiency, within-chain stationarity,
and the Monte Carlo precision of posterior estimates.

Agreement across chains was assessed using
rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$, with values close to
one indicating consistency between within-chain
and between-chain variability. Bulk and tail effective
sample sizes (ESS) were used to assess how much
information the correlated draws provided about
the centre and tails of the posterior distribution,
respectively \citep{vehtari2021rank}.

Post-burn-in divergences were also monitored, as they
signal numerical difficulties in following Hamiltonian
trajectories and may indicate that some posterior
regions have not been adequately explored
\citep{betancourt2017conceptual}.

The Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch diagnostics provided
additional checks on individual chains
\citep{geweke1992evaluating,heidelberger1983simulation}.

The Geweke diagnostic compares the means of an early
and a late segment of a chain, standardising their
difference using a standard error that accounts for
autocorrelation. Under suitable stationarity conditions,
the statistic has an asymptotic standard Normal
distribution, with large absolute values suggesting
inconsistency between the two segments.

The Heidelberger--Welch procedure first assesses
stationarity using a test based on the
Cramér--von Mises statistic. If necessary, the test
is repeated after removing progressively larger
initial portions of the chain, up to half of the
available draws. Once stationarity is accepted,
a half-width test checks whether the Monte Carlo
confidence interval for the posterior mean, computed
from the remaining draws, is sufficiently narrow
relative to the magnitude of the estimated mean.

Trace plots and autocorrelation functions (ACFs)
for selected parameters complemented these numerical
checks. Trace plots were inspected for persistent
trends, differences between chains, and whether the
chains explored similar posterior regions.
ACFs showed how dependence between draws changed
with increasing lag, with faster decay generally
indicating more efficient sampling
\citep{vehtari2021rank}.

\chapter{Results and discussion}
\label{chap:results}

\section{Rank-one model results}
\label{sec: rank-one model}
This section presents the posterior results for the terminal
model and the chokepoint model, under the rank-one factor
specification. Covariate associations, relationships among
responses, and the interpretation of the latent factor in each
case are discussed. Furthermore, posterior predictive checks
are performed for each type of node to assess how well the
model reproduces the observed responses distributions and
associations.

\subsection{Terminal posterior analysis}
\label{sec:terminal_results}

The rank-one terminal model was estimated using four MCMC chains,
each with 20,000 warm-up iterations and 30,000 retained draws.
The posterior estimates of the factor-regression coefficients,
computed as the mean of the retained MCMC samples,
are presented in Table~\ref{tab:simple_beta}, together with
their 95\% highest-density intervals (HDIs).

The most pronounced positive associations concern lagged
exporter status and terminal capacity. Conditional on the
other predictors, having been classified as an exporter
in the preceding month corresponds to an increase of
approximately 1.4 units in the expected factor value.
This is substantial relative to the residual standard
deviation of the factor, which is fixed at one.

Neighbour activity and voyage distance in the previous
month are also positively related to the factor, but
their estimated associations are considerably weaker.
By contrast, the posterior intervals for terminal age
and the seasonal terms include zero, leaving their
individual contributions uncertain.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Factor-regression coefficients for the terminal model.
Numeric predictors are standardised; exporter status is a
zero--one indicator.}
\label{tab:simple_beta}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Predictor & Posterior mean & 95\% HDI \\
\hline
Lagged neighbour activity share & 0.096 & $[0.058,0.134]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{lagged voyage distance})$ & 0.062 & $[0.022,0.102]$ \\
Month sine & $-0.032$ & $[-0.068,0.003]$ \\
Month cosine & $-0.003$ & $[-0.038,0.033]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{capacity})$ & 0.409 & $[0.367,0.452]$ \\
Terminal age & $-0.019$ & $[-0.057,0.019]$ \\
Lagged exporter indicator & 1.395 & $[1.303,1.489]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The response loadings in Table~\ref{tab:simple_loadings}
provide further insight into the interpretation of the
terminal factor, by describing associations with the
interior means of bounded responses, the probabilities
of binary outcomes, and the boundary probabilities.

The clearest pattern combines increasing PageRank with
decreasing counterparty concentration. In particular,
higher factor values correspond to both a lower interior
mean for HHI and a reduced probability of HHI being
exactly one.

The structural indicators show contrasting relationships:
the probability of being an articulation point increases
with the factor, while that of having an incident global
bridge decreases. For connection to an articulation point,
the posterior interval includes zero, providing no clear
evidence of an association.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Loadings on the latent factor. Bounded interior
means and all probabilities use logit links.}
\label{tab:simple_loadings}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Response component & Posterior mean & 95\% HDI \\
\hline
PageRank interior mean & 0.359 & $[0.348,0.370]$ \\
HHI interior mean & $-0.535$ & $[-0.567,-0.503]$ \\
Dependence interior mean & 0.420 & $[0.395,0.445]$ \\
Articulation-point probability & 0.770 & $[0.684,0.858]$ \\
Incident-global-bridge probability & $-0.638$ & $[-0.713,-0.563]$ \\
Connected-to-articulation probability & 0.109 & $[-0.014,0.235]$ \\
Probability of HHI equal to one & $-3.985$ & $[-4.699,-3.303]$ \\
Probability of dependence equal to one & $-0.545$ & $[-0.636,-0.451]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The magnitude of the HHI relationship becomes clearer
when expressed in the original response units. Moving
from a factor value of 0 to 1, the posterior median
expected HHI falls from approximately 0.66 to 0.38, with
a median difference of $-0.289$ (95\% equal-tailed
credible interval: $[-0.311,-0.266]$), incorporating
both the interior Beta component and the point mass at one.

Dependence exhibits a more complex pattern. Its
interior mean increases with the factor, whereas
the probability of an exact value of one declines.
Combining these components, as defined in
Equation~\eqref{eq:inflated_mean}, yields a mildly
U-shaped conditional expectation over the displayed
factor range.

The fitted response functions are displayed in
Figure~\ref{fig:simple_response_curves} to allow
the different response components and their
combined implications to be examined across
the estimated factor range.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/terminal_response_curves.png}
\caption{Fitted response functions against the terminal latent factor. Lines represent posterior medians, and shaded 
bands indicate equal-tailed 95\% credible intervals for conditional expected responses or probabilities. HHI and 
dependence means include the point mass at one. 
The horizontal axis spans the full empirical range of posterior mean latent factor scores in the estimation sample.}
\label{fig:simple_response_curves}
\end{figure}

To relate these posterior patterns to observed network positions, two terminal-month observations with 
posterior mean latent factor scores closest to the 5th and 95th percentiles of the estimation sample are 
considered. 
Mugardos LNG Terminal, located in Galicia, Spain, has an estimated latent factor score of approximately $-1.0$ in October 2022, 
compared with $2.73$ for Sabine Pass LNG Terminal, located in Cameron Parish, Louisiana, in June 2024. 
Their observed PageRank values are 0.011 and 0.039, respectively, while their counterparty HHI values are 1.00 and 0.17. 
Mugardos is classified as an importer and has an incident global bridge, whereas Sabine Pass is an exporter without one. 
These observations were selected to illustrate contrasting positions along the latent dimension 
while avoiding the most extreme cases, and display differences which are broadly consistent with the estimated relationships between the latent factor and the terminal 
responses presented above, although the two observations also differ in commercial role.

An additional case of interest is Rasgas LNG Terminal 3, Qatar, which exhibits the highest posterior mean latent factor 
score in the estimation sample, approximately $6.38$ in June 2020. Its posterior mean factor score can be decomposed into a systematic 
component $\mathbf{x}_n^\top\boldsymbol{\beta}\approx 1.84$
and a residual component $z_n\approx 4.55$, indicating a substantial deviation from the 
position explained by the observed covariates. 
Importantly, this deviation is not confined to a single observation: across its 59 terminal-month observations, Rasgas exhibits a positive posterior 
mean residual in every month, with an average of approximately $3.13$. Similar, although less pronounced, patterns are 
observed for other terminals, including Fos Tonkin, Yamal and Sabine Pass, whose residual components remain predominantly 
positive over time. Conversely, terminals such as Cameroon FLNG and Sakhalin II exhibit consistently negative posterior 
mean residuals. Table~\ref{tab:baseline_terminal_residuals} in the Appendix provides descriptive summaries of these 
patterns. 

To complement the observation-level examples, Figure~\ref{fig:terminal_factor_map} illustrates the geographical distribution 
of posterior mean latent factor scores averaged over time for each terminal in the estimation sample. 
The highest average factor scores are observed among major LNG export terminals located in geographically distinct regions, 
including the Middle East, North America, Russia and West Africa. This pattern is consistent with the strong positive association 
between lagged exporter status and the latent factor identified in Table~\ref{tab:simple_beta}. Conversely, terminals with the 
lowest average scores are also distributed across different geographical areas, with several located in East and Southeast Asia. 
Overall, the spatial distribution does not suggest that high or low factor values are confined to a single geographical region. 
Rather, the geographical representation reinforces the interpretation of the latent factor as a network-related dimension associated 
with terminal characteristics and commercial roles, rather than geographical location alone.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/terminal_mean_factor_map.pdf}
\caption{Geographical distribution of time-averaged posterior mean latent factor scores for LNG terminals under the baseline rank-one model. The colour scale is centred at zero.}
\label{fig:terminal_factor_map}
\end{figure}

The persistent residual patterns described above suggest that part of the variation left unexplained by the baseline 
covariates may reflect systematic differences across terminals rather than exclusively observation-specific fluctuations. 
This provides an empirical motivation for extending the rank-one model by introducing node-specific effects, 
allowing persistent terminal-level heterogeneity to be represented separately from residual variation. 
The results of this extension are presented in Section~\ref{sec:node_specific_model}.

While the preceding examples illustrate the interpretation of the latent factor and highlight potential limitations 
of the baseline specification, the extent to which the model reproduces the observed response patterns is examined 
through the posterior predictive checks in the following subsection.

\subsubsection{Terminal posterior predictive assessment}
\label{subsubsec:simple_ppc}

This section evaluates how well the
fitted model reproduces the observed response distributions
and associations by comparing statistics computed from
the terminal-month observations with those obtained from
replicated responses conditional on the posterior
factors. These comparisons are conducted both overall
and within lagged-role groups, with 95\% equal-tailed
intervals summarising the variability of the statistics across 200 conditional in-sample replications.

The model closely reproduces the HHI overall response means and the overall frequencies of dependence equal to one,
with the observed overall binary proportions also lying within their respective replicated intervals. 
Nevertheless, discrepancies emerge in the dispersion, quantiles and role-specific boundary frequencies of the bounded responses.
For PageRank, the model overestimates dispersion among lagged importers and underestimates it among lagged exporters. 
This difference is also reflected in the tails of the distributions: among importers, the observed 5th percentile (0.0089) exceeds the replicated 95\% 
interval of $[0.0062,0.0068]$, indicating that the model generates excessively low values, whereas among exporters, the observed 95th percentile (0.0714) 
exceeds the corresponding replicated interval of $[0.0587,0.0648]$, indicating an underestimation in the upper tail.
For HHI, the model underestimates both the frequency of values equal to one (14.86\%, included in a 95\% interval of $[13.48\%,16.23\%]$, 
compared with an observed 18.00\%) and the overall dispersion among exporters, where the observed mean 0.3103 exceeds its replicated interval $[0.2818,0.2993]$.
For dependence, the discrepancies differ between terminal roles. The observed frequency of dependence equal to one among importers (26.45\%) 
slightly exceeds the replicated interval of $[21.72\%,26.07\%]$, whereas the corresponding frequency among exporters (9.04\%) 
falls below the replicated interval of $[11.79\%,16.14\%]$. 
Moreover, the model underestimates dependence dispersion among importers and overestimates it among exporters. 
The observed standard deviations are 0.3574 and 0.2296, respectively, compared with replicated intervals of $[0.3256,0.3402]$ and $[0.2709,0.2882]$.
The complete marginal posterior predictive checks are reported in Table~\ref{tab:terminal_marginal_ppc_full} in the Appendix.

These findings indicate that, although the baseline model captures several features of the overall response distributions, 
it does not fully reproduce their role-specific dispersion, tail behaviour and boundary frequencies. 

Association between responses is assessed by comparing observed pairwise 
Spearman correlations with those obtained from 200 replicated datasets 
selected evenly across the four chains. Table~\ref{tab:joint_ppc} focuses 
on the three response pairs associated with the five largest absolute 
discrepancies between observed correlations and posterior predictive 
medians, reporting results for the full sample, importers, and exporters 
to allow systematic comparisons across terminal roles. 
The complete set of 45 pairwise correlation checks is provided in 
Table~\ref{tab:joint_ppc_full} in the Appendix.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{Selected joint posterior predictive checks. Entries are Spearman correlations. 
Replicated intervals use 200 conditional in-sample replicates.}
\label{tab:joint_ppc}
\begin{tabular}{llrrr}
\hline
Response pair & Lagged role & Observed & Rep. median & 95\% interval \\
\hline
AP--GB & All & 0.290 & -0.124 & $[-0.162,-0.095]$ \\
AP--GB & Importer & 0.137 & -0.037 & $[-0.082,0.003]$ \\
AP--GB & Exporter & 0.546 & -0.158 & $[-0.209,-0.102]$ \\
HHI--GB & All & 0.533 & 0.295 & $[0.248,0.335]$ \\
HHI--GB & Importer & 0.644 & 0.189 & $[0.137,0.226]$ \\
HHI--GB & Exporter & 0.398 & 0.336 & $[0.268,0.397]$ \\
DEP--GB & All & -0.066 & -0.070 & $[-0.113,-0.037]$ \\
DEP--GB & Importers	& -0.154 & -0.020 & $[-0.062,0.016]$ \\
DEP--GB & Exporters	& 0.195 & -0.118 & $[-0.186,-0.065]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The largest discrepancy concerns the association between articulation-point 
and incident-global-bridge status among exporters, for which the observed 
correlation is positive ($0.546$), whereas the posterior predictive median 
is negative ($-0.158$). The same sign reversal is observed for the full 
sample, while the replicated correlation among importers is closer to zero, 
although still negative. This indicates that the model fails to reproduce 
the positive association between these two indicators across terminal roles.
The association between counterparty HHI and incident-global-bridge status 
also exhibits substantial discrepancies, particularly among importers, for 
whom the observed correlation ($0.644$) considerably exceeds the posterior 
predictive median ($0.189$). In contrast, the corresponding discrepancy 
among exporters is relatively small. For dependence and incident-global-bridge 
status, the model reproduces the observed negative association reasonably 
well in the full sample but underestimates its magnitude among importers. 
Among exporters, the observed correlation is positive ($0.195$), whereas 
the posterior predictive median is negative ($-0.118$), indicating a further sign reversal.

These discrepancies suggest limitations in the rank-one model's ability to 
reproduce the dependence structure among responses. In particular, the single 
latent factor has opposite loadings on articulation-point and incident-global-bridge 
probabilities, while the assumption of conditional independence does not 
allow for additional residual associations between these responses. 
Consequently, the model struggles to reproduce their positive observed co-occurrence.
These findings provide a complementary motivation for considering a 
rank-two specification, in which two latent factors allow for a more 
flexible representation of associations among responses. The results of 
this extension are presented in Section~\ref{sec:rank_two_model}.

\subsection{Chokepoint posterior analysis}
\label{sec:chokepoint_findings}
This section examines how the latent factor relates to network characteristics and measures of centrality, flow exposure, and structural importance for the chokepoint specification. The adequacy of the fitted model is subsequently evaluated through posterior predictive checks.

For chokepoints, the factor-regression results in Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_beta} indicate relatively modest associations with the available predictors. Greater neighbour activity and longer average voyage distances in the preceding month are associated with higher factor values; both posterior intervals exclude zero, although the lower endpoint for voyage distance is close to zero. The seasonal coefficients provide limited evidence of seasonal variation in the conditional factor mean: the interval for the negative cosine coefficient excludes zero, whereas the interval for the sine coefficient includes zero.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Factor-regression coefficients for the chokepoint model. Numeric predictors are standardised. Intervals are 94\% highest-density intervals (HDIs), as exported by ArviZ.}
\label{tab:chokepoint_beta}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Predictor & Posterior mean & 94\% HDI \\
\hline
Lagged neighbour activity share & 0.103 & $[0.042,0.162]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{lagged voyage distance})$ & 0.065 & $[0.004,0.123]$ \\
Month sine & $-0.036$ & $[-0.096,0.021]$ \\
Month cosine & $-0.065$ & $[-0.123,-0.007]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

With respect to the responses, Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_loadings} shows positive loadings across the interior means and binary structural probabilities, with all reported HDIs excluding zero. These relationships are further illustrated by the fitted response curves in Figure~\ref{fig:chokepoint_response_curves}.

The factor is positively related to PageRank, betweenness, and flow share, indicating that higher scores correspond to chokepoints occupying more central positions and carrying larger proportions of the network's LNG flows. Both articulation-point status and connection to an articulation point become more probable as the factor increases, unlike the terminal specification, where the latter association is not clearly established. The negative loading on the probability of exact zero betweenness implies that higher factor values are associated with a lower probability of zero betweenness, reinforcing the positive relationship between the factor and the overall conditional expectation of this response.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Loadings on the latent factor for the chokepoint model. Bounded interior means and all probabilities use logit links. Intervals are 94\% HDIs, as exported by ArviZ.}
\label{tab:chokepoint_loadings}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Response component & Posterior mean & 94\% HDI \\
\hline
PageRank interior mean & 0.263 & $[0.244,0.281]$ \\
Betweenness interior mean & 1.024 & $[0.971,1.079]$ \\
Flow-share interior mean & 0.619 & $[0.580,0.658]$ \\
Articulation-point probability & 0.719 & $[0.566,0.876]$ \\
Connected-to-articulation probability & 1.206 & $[0.945,1.461]$ \\
Probability of betweenness equal to zero & $-1.658$ & $[-2.536,-0.797]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/chokepoint_response_curves.png}
\caption{Fitted response functions against the chokepoint latent factor. Lines are posterior medians and bands are equal-tailed 95\% credible intervals for conditional expected responses or binary probabilities. The betweenness mean includes the mass at zero. The horizontal axis spans the full empirical range of posterior mean latent factor scores in the estimation sample.}
\label{fig:chokepoint_response_curves}
\end{figure}

To illustrate how these relationships are reflected in individual observations, consider Cape of Good Hope in May 2021 and Taiwan Strait 
in October 2021. Their posterior mean factor values, located near the 5th and 95th percentiles of the estimation sample, are approximately 
$-1.63$ and $1.55$, respectively. Taiwan Strait has substantially higher observed PageRank (0.035 versus 0.007), betweenness 
(0.040 versus 0.0006), and flow share (3.6\% versus 0.3\%). Nevertheless, both chokepoint-month observations are classified as 
articulation points and are connected to articulation points. Their different factor values therefore reflect variation in the 
continuous measures even though these binary structural indicators coincide, illustrating how the estimated factor summarises different 
aspects of chokepoint centrality, flow exposure, and structural position.

Although the chokepoint latent factor does not exhibit values as extreme as those observed for some terminals, a similar pattern of 
persistent residual deviations emerges. The highest and lowest posterior mean factor scores occur for Gibraltar Strait in November 2024 
($2.394$) and Sunda Strait in December 2022 ($-2.282$), respectively. In both cases, the systematic components explained by the 
covariates are close to zero ($-0.014$ and $0.044$), whereas the posterior mean residual components account for nearly the entire factor 
scores ($2.408$ and $-2.326$, respectively).

As observed for the terminals, these deviations are not limited to isolated months. Gibraltar Strait exhibits positive posterior mean 
residuals in 96.6\% of its 59 observations, with an average of $1.294$, whereas Sunda Strait exhibits negative residuals in 95.9\% of 
its 49 observations, averaging $-1.179$. Similar patterns are found across other chokepoints: among the 20 with at least 20 observations, 
13 exhibit residuals of the same sign in more than 85\% of their observed months. Descriptive summaries of these cases are provided in 
Appendix~\ref{app:chokepoint_residuals}, Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_residual_summary}.

The five highest and five lowest time-averaged scores are highlighted  in Figure ~\ref{fig:chokepoint_factor_map} to illustrate the spatial persistent variations in the estimated latent dimension.
The highest average factor scores are associated with Gibraltar Strait, Taiwan Strait, Suez Canal, Malacca Strait, and Bab el-Mandeb Strait, 
all located along major maritime corridors connecting Europe, the Middle East, and Asia. 
Conversely, the lowest average scores are observed for Mona Passage and Windward Passage in the Caribbean, Magellan Strait at the 
southern tip of South America, and Torres Strait and Sunda Strait in the Indo-Pacific region.
Notably, the coexistence of relatively high and low factor scores among geographically proximate chokepoints in Southeast Asia suggests 
that geographical proximity does not necessarily correspond to similar positions along the estimated latent dimension.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/chokepoint_mean_factor_map.pdf}
\caption{Geographical distribution of time-averaged posterior mean latent factor scores for chokepoints under the baseline rank-one model. The colour scale is centred at zero.}
\label{fig:chokepoint_factor_map}
\end{figure}

The persistent residual patterns identified above reinforce the evidence of node-level heterogeneity already observed for terminals, 
suggesting that the baseline covariates may not fully account for systematic differences across chokepoints and providing further motivation for the node-specific extension discussed in Section~\ref{sec:node_specific_model}.

\subsubsection{Chokepoint posterior predictive assessment}

Following the same conditional, in-sample approach used for terminals, the chokepoint posterior predictive checks indicate that the model reproduces overall response means and binary proportions reasonably closely.

The observed PageRank mean (0.01764) is close to the replicated mean (0.01767), and its observed 5th, median, and 95th percentiles fall within their respective replicated intervals. The model also reproduces the low frequency of zero betweenness (0.516\% observed versus 0.584\% replicated). Nevertheless, the observed fifth percentile of betweenness (0.000575) lies just below the corresponding replicated interval $[0.000582,0.000988]$; its observed median (0.007677) is also slightly below the replicated interval $[0.007731,0.008545]$.

The largest marginal discrepancies concern flow share. The observed standard deviation (0.02010) exceeds the 95\% replicated interval $[0.01693,0.01933]$, indicating that the model underestimates dispersion. The observed median (0.01589) falls below its replicated interval $[0.01796,0.02040]$, whereas the observed 95th percentile (0.06426) exceeds the corresponding interval $[0.05522,0.06304]$. These discrepancies indicate that, despite reproducing the overall mean (0.02341 observed versus 0.02354 replicated), the model does not fully capture the distribution of flow share.

Posterior predictive checks also examine whether the model reproduces the observed associations between responses. Pairwise Spearman correlations are compared with those obtained from 200 replicated datasets selected evenly across the four chains. As shown in Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_joint_ppc}, the model reproduces the PageRank--betweenness association reasonably closely but underestimates associations involving flow share, as well as the PageRank--articulation association. The observed PageRank--articulation correlation lies slightly above the upper endpoint of the replicated interval.

Response pair & Observed & Rep. median & 95\% interval \\
\hline

PageRank--flow share & 0.672 & 0.531 & $[0.481,0.569]$ \\
