\subsection{Sampling diagnostics}
\label{subsec:baseline_sampling_diagnostics}

Sampling performance was evaluated using rank-normalised
$\widehat{R}$, bulk and tail effective sample sizes (ESS),
post-burn-in divergences, and the Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch diagnostics. 
The Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch diagnostics were applied separately to each chain for every global parameter in both node-specific models, 
using the retained posterior draws.
Observation-level latent factors were excluded from these additional tests 
to limit the computational burden associated with evaluating thousands of observation-specific 
quantities across four chains. Their convergence and sampling efficiency were nevertheless 
assessed through rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$ and bulk and tail effective sample sizes.

Neither model presented post-burn-in divergences. 
The maximum rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$, rounded to three 
decimal places, was 1.001 for terminals and 1.003 for chokepoints.

For terminals, the minimum bulk ESS
was 2,931, associated with the
lagged exporter coefficient, and the minimum tail ESS was 6,726 for the HHI boundary loading.
For chokepoints, the corresponding values were
1,693 and 3,698, both associated with the
betweenness precision parameter, indicating satisfactory convergence
and sampling efficiency.

Trace plots and within-chain autocorrelation functions are presented in
Appendix~\ref{app:mcmc_diagnostics} and
provide additional visual evidence of sampling
behaviour. In particular, the betweenness loading
and precision parameters exhibit relatively
persistent autocorrelation in the chokepoint model,
consistent with their comparatively lower effective sample sizes.

The Geweke diagnostic was used to assess differences between 
the early and late portions of each chain. For the terminal model, 
92 out of 104 parameter-chain tests (88.5\%) satisfied $|Z|\leq 1.96$. 
The remaining 12 tests exceeded the threshold, with a maximum absolute $Z$-score of 3.07.

For the chokepoint model, 71 out of 76 tests (93.4\%) satisfied the same 
criterion. The five exceedances were concentrated primarily among the 
precision parameters of the continuous responses, with one additional 
exceedance involving the betweenness boundary-component loading. 
The maximum absolute $Z$-score was 2.46.

Although these results indicate some differences
between the early and late portions of individual
chains, the tests were conducted separately for
multiple parameters and chains without adjustment
for multiple comparisons. Consequently, isolated
exceedances should not be interpreted as evidence of non-convergence,
particularly in the presence of satisfactory
$\widehat{R}$, ESS, and divergence diagnostics.

The Heidelberger--Welch procedure was used
to assess stationarity and the Monte Carlo
precision of posterior mean estimates.

All 104 terminal and 76 chokepoint
parameter-chain tests satisfied the
stationarity criterion. For terminals, 93 tests passed without
excluding any additional draws, while
11 required the exclusion of between
1,000 and 3,000 initial draws.
For chokepoints, 74 tests passed without
additional exclusions, while the remaining
two required the exclusion of 3,000 and
4,000 draws, respectively.

The half-width test assessed whether
the half-width of the 95\% Monte Carlo
confidence interval for the posterior
mean was below 10\% of its absolute
estimated value.
All 76 chokepoint parameter-chain tests satisfied
this criterion. For terminals,
96 out of 104 tests (92.3\%)
passed, while the remaining eight
failures involved only two parameters:
the seasonal regression coefficient
associated with $\mathrm{month\_cos}$
and the continuous intercept for
counterparty HHI, each across all
four chains.
Notably, both parameters had posterior means
close to zero, making the relative
half-width criterion particularly
sensitive to small absolute differences.
These failures therefore do not
necessarily indicate substantial
Monte Carlo uncertainty in absolute
terms.

Table~\ref{tab:baseline_sampling_diagnostics} summarises the convergence and mixing diagnostics, 
together with the results of the Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch tests for both baseline models.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Summary of sampling diagnostics for the
baseline terminal and chokepoint models.
Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch results refer
to parameter-chain tests on distinct global parameters.}
\label{tab:baseline_sampling_diagnostics}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\hline
Diagnostic & Terminals & Chokepoints \\
\hline

\hline

Additional results are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:mcmc_diagnostics}.

Overall, the combined diagnostics indicate
satisfactory sampling behaviour for both
baseline models.
The absence of divergences, $\widehat{R}$
values close to one, and sufficiently large
effective sample sizes provide complementary
evidence of satisfactory posterior exploration
and sampling efficiency.

\section{Node-specific intercept model results}
\label{sec:node_specific_model}

This section presents the posterior results of the
rank-one model extended with node-specific random
intercepts.
The analysis focuses on the magnitude of
between-node heterogeneity, changes in the
factor-regression coefficients, and the estimated
node-specific effects. The response loadings are
also examined to assess whether the interpretation
of the latent factor changes under the extended
specification. Finally, posterior predictive checks
and sampling diagnostics are reported.

Both models were estimated using four MCMC chains,
each with 20,000 warm-up iterations and 30,000
retained draws. The results for terminals and
chokepoints are presented separately, consistently
with the baseline analysis.

\subsection{Terminal posterior analysis}
\label{subsec:node_terminal_results}

The posterior distribution of the random-effect
scale parameter provides evidence of substantial
persistent heterogeneity across terminals.
The posterior mean of $\sigma_\mu$ is 1.373,
with a 94\% highest-density interval (HDI) of
$[1.193,1.565]$.
Since the residual variance of the latent factor
remains fixed at one, this estimate indicates
that persistent between-terminal differences
represent an important component of the
variation in latent factor scores, beyond
that explained by the observed covariates.

The introduction of node-specific intercepts
also changes the estimated associations between
the covariates and the latent factor.
Table~\ref{tab:node_terminal_beta} reports
the posterior estimates alongside those
obtained under the baseline specification.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Factor-regression coefficients for the
terminal random-intercept model, compared with
the baseline rank-one specification.
Numeric predictors are standardised;
exporter status is a zero--one indicator.}
\label{tab:node_terminal_beta}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\hline
Predictor & Baseline mean & Node mean & Node 94\% HDI \\
\hline
Lagged neighbour activity
& 0.096 & 0.016 & $[-0.039,0.072]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{lagged voyage distance})$
& 0.062 & 0.053 & $[-0.022,0.129]$ \\
Month sine
& $-0.032$ & $-0.038$ & $[-0.080,0.004]$ \\
Month cosine
& $-0.003$ & 0.004 & $[-0.038,0.045]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{capacity})$
& 0.409 & 0.725 & $[0.493,0.957]$ \\
Terminal age
& $-0.019$ & $-0.326$ & $[-0.558,-0.089]$ \\
Lagged exporter indicator
& 1.395 & 1.309 & $[0.823,1.783]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Compared with the baseline specification,
the coefficient associated with lagged
neighbour activity decreases from 0.096
to 0.016, and its posterior interval
now includes zero. The coefficient for
lagged voyage distance remains positive
but is less precisely estimated, with
its interval also including zero.
These changes suggest that some of the
associations identified under the baseline
model may have reflected persistent
differences between terminals that are
now represented by the random intercepts.
In contrast, terminal capacity and lagged exporter
status remain positively associated with the latent
factor even after accounting for unobserved
terminal-specific heterogeneity.
However, their posterior intervals
are considerably wider than under
the baseline specification, indicating
greater uncertainty in separating
covariate associations from persistent
node-specific differences.
A further change concerns terminal age:
whereas its posterior interval included
zero under the baseline specification,
the random-intercept model yields a
negative posterior mean of $-0.326$,
with a 94\% HDI of $[-0.558,-0.089]$,
indicating a negative conditional
association between terminal age and
the latent factor.

The response loadings reported in Table~\ref{tab:node_terminal_loadings} 
retain the main directional relationships identified
under the baseline specification, although the magnitudes of several estimates
are smaller.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Posterior estimates of response
loadings for the terminal random-intercept
model. Bounded interior means and
probabilities use logit links.
Intervals are 94\% HDIs.}
\label{tab:node_terminal_loadings}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Response component & Posterior mean & 94\% HDI \\
\hline
PageRank interior mean
& 0.197 & $[0.189,0.206]$ \\
HHI interior mean
& $-0.283$ & $[-0.301,-0.266]$ \\
Dependence interior mean
& 0.232 & $[0.217,0.248]$ \\
Articulation-point probability
& 0.394 & $[0.347,0.441]$ \\
Incident-global-bridge probability
& $-0.311$ & $[-0.351,-0.271]$ \\
Connected-to-articulation probability
& 0.096 & $[0.021,0.172]$ \\
Probability of HHI equal to one
& $-1.872$ & $[-2.141,-1.618]$ \\
Probability of dependence equal to one
& $-0.318$ & $[-0.378,-0.262]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Higher factor values remain associated
with higher PageRank, lower counterparty
concentration, and a greater probability
of being an articulation point.
The probability of having an incident
global bridge continues to decrease
with the factor.
As in the baseline model, dependence
exhibits opposing relationships between
its interior and boundary components:
the interior mean increases with the
factor, whereas the probability of
dependence equal to one decreases.
One difference concerns connection to an articulation point: 
its loading remains positive and its
94\% HDI now excludes zero, whereas
the corresponding interval included
zero under the baseline specification.

In order to provide a direct representation of persistent terminal-level heterogeneity, Figure~\ref{fig:node_terminal_effects} 
illustrates the posterior distributions of the node-specific intercepts for the five terminals with the highest and five with the lowest posterior mean effects, 
restricting the selection to terminals with at least 20 observations in the estimation sample. 
Findings are generally consistent with the persistent residual patterns identified under the baseline model: 
the largest positive posterior mean effect is associated with Rasgas LNG Terminal 3, approximately 7.615,
with a 95\% equal-tailed credible interval of $[6.942,8.320]$.
Other terminals exhibiting substantial positive effects include Fos Tonkin LNG Terminal (3.69), Yamal LNG Terminal (3.18), Sabine Pass LNG Terminal (3.11), and 
Pluto LNG Terminal (3.10), all of which were also identified in the baseline analysis as exhibiting predominantly positive latent-factor residuals.
Their positive random intercepts therefore suggest that the systematic deviations observed under the baseline specification can, at least partly, 
be represented by persistent node-level differences.
At the opposite end, Sakhalin II LNG Terminal and Cameroon FLNG Terminal exhibit the most negative effects among the selected terminals, 
consistent with the predominantly negative residuals identified in the baseline analysis. 
Oman Qalhat LNG Terminal, Punta Europa LNG Terminal, and Elba Island LNG Terminal also display negative node-specific intercepts, indicating
that their latent factor values tend to be lower than those predicted by the observed covariates alone.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/terminal_node_effects_selected.pdf}
    \caption{Posterior distributions of selected terminal-specific random intercepts under the rank-one model. The five largest and five smallest posterior mean effects are shown among terminals with at least 20 observations in the estimation sample. Points represent posterior means, and horizontal bars indicate 95\% equal-tailed credible intervals. The dashed vertical line denotes zero.}
    \label{fig:node_terminal_effects}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Posterior predictive assessment}

Posterior predictive checks were conducted to examine whether the
introduction of node-specific intercepts
improves the reproduction of marginal
response distributions and associations
between responses.
The same conditional, in-sample
approach adopted for the baseline model was used:
observed response statistics were compared
with those obtained from 200 replicated
datasets generated conditional on posterior
latent factor values.
Table~\ref{tab:node_terminal_marginal_ppc} shows that the random-intercept model closely reproduces the overall means of the observed terminal responses.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Marginal posterior predictive checks for the terminal random-intercept model. 
Observed and replicated means are reported on the original response scale, rounded to five decimal places highlight differences. 
For binary responses, means correspond to proportions.}
\label{tab:node_terminal_marginal_ppc}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Response & Observed mean & Replicated mean \\
\hline
PageRank & 0.01852 & 0.01854 \\
HHI & 0.58872 & 0.58933 \\
Dependence & 0.49478 & 0.49618 \\
Articulation point (AP) & 0.10571 & 0.10637 \\
Incident global bridge (GB) & 0.58500 & 0.58509 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

However, as in the baseline model, agreement in overall means does not necessarily imply adequate reproduction of dispersion, 
tail behaviour, or associations between responses.
In particular, the joint posterior predictive checks reveal that several discrepancies identified under the baseline specification persist.
Table~\ref{tab} compares selected response pairs exhibiting these discrepancies under the baseline and random-intercept models, allowing an 
assessment of whether introducing node-specific intercepts improves the model's ability to reproduce the observed associations.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{Selected joint posterior predictive
checks for terminals. Entries are Spearman
correlations.}
\label{tab:joint_ppc_node_intercepts}
\begin{tabular}{llrrr}
\hline
Response pair & Model & Observed
& Rep. median & 95\% interval \\
\hline
AP--GB & Baseline
& 0.290 & $-0.124$ & $[-0.162,-0.095]$ \\
AP--GB & Node intercept
& 0.290 & $-0.108$ & $[-0.143,-0.070]$ \\
HHI--GB & Baseline
& 0.533 & 0.295 & $[0.248,0.335]$ \\
HHI--GB & Node intercept
& 0.533 & 0.242 & $[0.204,0.289]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The most pronounced discrepancy continues to concern the association
between articulation-point and incident-global-bridge status, which the model still
fails to reproduce.
The discrepancy is particularly
pronounced among exporters.
Their observed AP--GB correlation
is 0.546, compared with a replicated
median of $-0.150$ under the
random-intercept model.
The association between HHI and
incident-global-bridge status also
remains underestimated.
For the full terminal sample,
the observed correlation is 0.533,
whereas the replicated median
decreases from 0.295 under the
baseline specification to 0.242
under the random-intercept model.

Thus, introducing node-specific
intercepts does not uniformly
improve the reproduction of joint
response associations.
This finding is consistent with
the structure of the extension.
The random intercepts account for
persistent heterogeneity in the
common latent factor, but the
model retains a single latent
dimension and conditional
independence of the responses
given that factor.
Consequently, the additional
node-specific variation does not
necessarily provide the flexibility
required to reproduce associations
that are poorly represented by
the rank-one structure.

Overall, the random-intercept
specification provides a more
explicit representation of persistent
between-terminal heterogeneity,
while preserving the main directional
relationships between the latent
factor and the observed responses.
Nevertheless, the posterior predictive
results indicate that important
limitations of the baseline rank-one
specification remain, particularly
in reproducing the joint occurrence
of articulation-point and
incident-global-bridge status.

The complete marginal and joint
posterior predictive results are
reported in the Appendix~\ref{app:ppc_node_intercept}.

\subsection{Chokepoint posterior analysis}
\label{subsec:node_chokepoint_results}

The random-intercept extension also
identifies substantial persistent
heterogeneity across chokepoints.
The posterior mean of the random-effect
scale parameter is 2.377, with a 94\%
HDI of $[1.806,2.973]$.
As for terminals, the estimated
random-effect scale suggests that
persistent differences between
chokepoints contribute substantially
to variation in the latent factor
beyond that associated with the
observed covariates.
The posterior estimates of the
factor-regression coefficients
are reported in
Table~\ref{tab:node_chokepoint_beta} and suggest that the
introduction of node-specific effects
changes the relative contributions
of the observed covariates.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Factor-regression coefficients
for the chokepoint random-intercept
model, compared with the baseline
rank-one specification.
Numeric predictors are standardised.
Intervals for the random-intercept
model are 94\% HDIs.}
\label{tab:node_chokepoint_beta}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\hline
Predictor & Baseline mean & Node mean & Node 94\% HDI \\
\hline
Lagged neighbour activity
& 0.103 & 0.316 & $[0.229,0.401]$ \\
$\log(1+\text{lagged voyage distance})$
& 0.065 & 0.088 & $[-0.067,0.244]$ \\
Month sine
& $-0.036$ & $-0.107$ & $[-0.175,-0.040]$ \\
Month cosine
& $-0.065$ & $-0.085$ & $[-0.152,-0.018]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The coefficients associated with
lagged neighbour activity increases
from 0.103 under the baseline model
to 0.316 under the random-intercept
specification, with a posterior
interval excluding zero.
The coefficient for lagged voyage
distance also remains positive, but
its posterior interval includes zero,
indicating greater uncertainty
about its conditional association
with the latent factor.
Both seasonal coefficients are
negative, with their reported
HDIs excluding zero, which differs from the baseline
specification, where only the
cosine coefficient had an interval excluding zero.
Given the sinusoidal encoding of calendar months, the combination of these negative coefficients implies 
a cyclical pattern in the expected latent factor, with lower values around February and higher values around August.

Turning to the response loadings, Table~\ref{tab:node_chokepoint_loadings} shows that the estimated relationships between the latent factor and the response loadings retain
the directions observed under the baseline model.
\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Posterior estimates of response
loadings for the chokepoint random-intercept
model. Bounded interior means and
probabilities use logit links.
Intervals are 94\% HDIs.}
\label{tab:node_chokepoint_loadings}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\hline
Response component & Posterior mean & 94\% HDI \\
\hline
PageRank interior mean
& 0.107 & $[0.097,0.117]$ \\
Betweenness interior mean
& 0.303 & $[0.280,0.327]$ \\
Flow-share interior mean
& 0.382 & $[0.350,0.414]$ \\
Articulation-point probability
& 0.362 & $[0.289,0.435]$ \\
Connected-to-articulation probability
& 0.354 & $[0.253,0.457]$ \\
Probability of betweenness equal to zero
& $-1.048$ & $[-1.576,-0.532]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}
Higher latent factor values correspond
to higher interior means for PageRank,
betweenness, and flow share, as well
as higher probabilities of being an
articulation point and being connected
to an articulation point.
The negative loading on the
zero-betweenness component indicates
that higher factor values are associated
with a lower probability of observing
exactly zero betweenness.
Thus, the estimated factor continues
to represent a dimension associated
with chokepoint centrality, flow
exposure, and structural importance.

The node-specific intercepts provide an additional perspective on the heterogeneity captured by the random-intercept specification. 
Figure~\ref{fig:node_chokepoint_effects} presents their posterior distributions for all 24 chokepoints, ordered by posterior mean.
The estimates reveal considerable heterogeneity across maritime passages, with both positive and negative effects 
relative to the covariate-dependent component of the latent factor.
The Strait of Hormuz exhibits the largest positive node-specific effect, with a posterior mean of approximately 4.79. 
Other substantial positive effects are associated with Malacca Strait (3.51), Gibraltar Strait (3.50), Taiwan Strait (3.13), 
Dover Strait (2.85), Suez Canal (2.73), and Bab el-Mandeb Strait (2.54). Indeed, several of these passages occupy strategically 
important positions along major international shipping corridors. 
Conversely, Torres Strait exhibits the most negative effect (approximately $-4.07$), followed by Windward Passage ($-3.75$), 
Magellan Strait ($-3.73$), Mona Passage ($-3.17$), and Sunda Strait ($-2.25$). These negative intercepts indicate systematically 
lower latent factor values relative to the covariate-based predictions, a pattern which is broadly consistent with the lower factor 
scores observed for several of these passages in the baseline specification.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{images/chokepoint_node_effects_all.pdf}
    \caption{Posterior distributions of the node-specific random intercepts for all 24 chokepoints under the rank-one model. Points represent posterior means, and horizontal bars indicate 95\% equal-tailed credible intervals. The dashed vertical line denotes zero. Estimates are preliminary due to unresolved sampling diagnostics.}
    \label{fig:node_chokepoint_effects}
\end{figure}

Although the posterior summaries
indicate substantial heterogeneity
across chokepoints, the convergence diagnostics discussed
in Section~\ref{subsec:node_sampling_diagnostics} raise concerns about the reliability of some estimates. 
The corresponding interpretations should therefore be treated with caution.

\subsubsection{Posterior predictive assessment}

To assess whether the random-intercept specification adequately captures the observed characteristics of the chokepoint network, 
posterior predictive checks were conducted using 200 conditional in-sample replicates. As for the preceeding models, 
the assessment considers both the marginal distributions of individual responses and the associations between them.

At the marginal level, the model closely reproduces the overall response means. 
The observed PageRank mean is 0.01764, compared with a replicated mean of 0.01765. 
For betweenness, the corresponding values are 0.01321 and 0.01323, while for flow share they are 0.02341 and 0.02342.

Nevertheless, discrepancies remain in the associations between responses. Table~\ref{tab:node_chokepoint_joint_ppc} 
reports four illustrative response pairs involving different combinations of centrality, flow exposure, 
and structural importance measures, for which the observed Spearman correlations exceed the upper bounds of 
the 95\% posterior predictive intervals, highlighting the model's tendency to underestimate certain positive associations.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{Selected joint posterior
predictive checks for chokepoints
under the random-intercept model.
Entries are Spearman correlations.
Replicated intervals are based on
200 conditional in-sample replicates.}
\label{tab:node_chokepoint_joint_ppc}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\hline
Response pair & Observed
& Rep. median & 95\% interval \\
\hline
PageRank--betweenness
& 0.685 & 0.473 & $[0.417,0.516]$ \\
PageRank--flow share
& 0.672 & 0.609 & $[0.562,0.644]$ \\
Betweenness--flow share
& 0.800 & 0.700 & $[0.659,0.739]$ \\
PageRank--articulation
& 0.287 & 0.201 & $[0.140,0.271]$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The largest discrepancy among the selected associations concerns PageRank and betweenness, 
for which the replicated median correlation is 0.473, compared with an observed value of 0.685. 
This represents a deterioration relative to the baseline specification, which yielded a replicated median of approximately 0.655.
The model also underestimates the positive associations between PageRank and flow share, 
betweenness and flow share, and PageRank and articulation-point status, indicating that introducing node-specific intercepts does not fully resolve the limitations 
of the baseline rank-one model in reproducing the observed dependence structure among chokepoint responses.
The complete marginal and joint posterior predictive results are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:node_intercept_chokepoints}.

\subsection{Sampling diagnostics}
\label{subsec:node_sampling_diagnostics}

Sampling performance was assessed using the same diagnostic procedures as for the baseline specification.
Table~\ref{tab:node_sampling_diagnostics} summarises the main results.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Sampling diagnostics for the terminal and chokepoint
random-intercept models. Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch
results refer to parameter-chain tests for global parameters.}
\label{tab:node_sampling_diagnostics}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\hline
Diagnostic & Terminals & Chokepoints \\
\hline

Post-burn-in divergences & 0 & 183 \\

Maximum $\widehat{R}$
& 1.003$^{a}$ & 1.040 \\
& & \scriptsize{betweenness intercept} \\

Minimum bulk ESS
& 795 & 112 \\
& \scriptsize{PageRank intercept}
& \scriptsize{flow-share intercept} \\

Minimum tail ESS
& 1,734 & 202 \\
& \scriptsize{PageRank intercept}
& \scriptsize{betweenness intercept} \\

\hline

Passed Geweke & 115 (95.8\%) & 85 (92.4\%) \\
Failed Geweke & 5 (4.2\%) & 7 (7.6\%) \\

\hline

Passed stationarity & 120 (100\%) & 92 (100\%) \\
Passed half-width & 113 (94.2\%) & 92 (100\%) \\

\hline
\end{tabular}

\vspace{0.2cm}
\begin{minipage}{0.95\textwidth}
\footnotesize
The maximum reported $\widehat{R}$ for terminals
is shared by the PageRank interior-mean intercept,
the HHI interior-mean intercept, and the HHI
boundary-component intercept.
\end{minipage}

\end{table}

The terminal model exhibits
satisfactory overall sampling
behaviour: no post-burn-in divergences
were recorded, and the maximum
rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$
was 1.003.
The minimum bulk and tail ESS
were 795 and 1,734, respectively, and although these values are lower
than those obtained under the
baseline specification, they
remain compatible with adequate
posterior exploration for the
reported summaries.
The Geweke diagnostic was
satisfied by 115 of the 120
parameter-chain tests, all tests passed the
Heidelberger--Welch stationarity criterion, and the half-width test was
passed by 113 of the 120 tests.

Conversely, the chokepoint model exhibited less satisfactory sampling diagnostics. 
Although 85 of the 92 parameter-chain tests satisfied the Geweke criterion, and all tests passed the 
Heidelberger--Welch stationarity and half-width criteria, a total of 183 post-warm-up divergences were 
recorded, with 168 concentrated in one chain and 15 in another. The maximum rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$ 
was 1.040, while the minimum bulk and tail ESS were 112 and 202, respectively.
These results suggest incomplete exploration of the posterior distribution, potentially associated 
with difficult posterior geometry and insufficient mixing. In particular, the presence of divergences, 
together with the low ESS values, indicates that the available posterior draws 
may not adequately represent the target distribution.
Consequently, the chokepoint posterior summaries should be interpreted cautiously, as the reported 
credible intervals and posterior predictive results may be affected by incomplete posterior exploration.

Additional trace plots, autocorrelation functions, and detailed diagnostic results are provided in Appendix~\ref{app:node_intercept_mcmc_diagnostics}.

\section{Rank-two model results}
\label{sec:rank_two_model}

The rank-two specifications were estimated to investigate whether introducing a second latent dimension could capture patterns of network criticality that were not adequately represented by the baseline rank-one and random-intercept models. The posterior results, however, provide limited evidence of a clearly identifiable additional dimension, particularly in light of the substantial sampling difficulties discussed below.

For terminals, the factor-regression coefficients exhibit considerable uncertainty, with most 94\% highest-density intervals including zero. In contrast to the baseline rank-one specification, the previously strong positive associations with terminal capacity and lagged exporter status are not clearly recovered. The response loadings are similarly uncertain, with many intervals spanning zero, making it difficult to identify distinct interpretations for the two factors. The posterior predictive checks also indicate that several discrepancies identified under the simpler specifications persist. In particular, the positive association between articulation-point and incident-global-bridge indicators remains underestimated, with an observed Spearman correlation of 0.290 compared with a replicated median of 0.166. The association between PageRank and dependence is likewise underestimated (0.314 versus 0.196).

For chokepoints, the factor-regression coefficients are generally small and uncertain, with no clear evidence of systematic associations between the available covariates and either latent dimension. The response loadings suggest a stronger relationship between the first factor and PageRank, betweenness, and monthly flow share, whereas the second-factor loadings are considerably less stable. Descriptive correlations between posterior mean factor coordinates and observed responses are consistent with this distinction, although they should not be interpreted as independent evidence of model adequacy. Compared with the baseline rank-one model, the joint posterior predictive checks show closer agreement for several response pairs. For example, the observed betweenness–flow-share correlation of 0.800 is reproduced with a median of 0.785, while the PageRank–flow-share correlation of 0.672 has a replicated median of 0.646. Nevertheless, some discrepancies remain, including the association between PageRank and articulation-point status.

The sampling diagnostics raise substantial concerns for both specifications. The terminal model exhibits 8,096 post-warm-up divergences, a maximum $\widehat{R}$ of 1.642, and a minimum bulk ESS of 6.8. The corresponding chokepoint model exhibits 2,630 divergences, a maximum $\widehat{R}$ of 1.372, and a minimum bulk ESS of 9.1. In both cases, the poorest mixing is associated with latent-factor coordinates and response loadings, indicating difficulties in adequately exploring the posterior distribution. These findings are also reflected in the Geweke and Heidelberger--Welch diagnostics, particularly for terminals.

Overall, although the rank-two specification appears to improve the reproduction of some joint response relationships, especially for chokepoints, the severe convergence and mixing difficulties prevent a reliable assessment of whether the additional latent dimension provides a meaningful improvement over the baseline and random-intercept specifications.

\section{Prior sensitivity results}
\label{sec:prior_sensitivity}
To assess the implications of the prior assumptions and the sensitivity of posterior
findings, two alternative prior profiles were investigated on the same estimation samples.
The analysis considers both the prior predictive distributions and the sensitivity
of posterior inference to these modifications.

\subsection{Alternative prior specifications}
Three prior specifications were considered: the original specification (ORG), described above; 
a concentrated specification (CON), with more concentrated priors for selected parameters; and a response-specific precision specification (RS), 
allowing prior distributions to vary across response variables.
The alternative specifications were informed by the observed response distributions and prior predictive
simulations, rather than independently elicited prior information.

Under the original priors, prior predictive simulations generated relatively broad
distributions for several network measures, particularly those characterised by small
observed values, such as PageRank, betweenness and flow share.
In response to these findings, the second specification introduced more concentrated
prior distributions for selected intercepts and factor loadings. Specifically, the standard
deviations of the logit-intercept priors for the small-valued continuous responses
were reduced from 1.5 to 0.7, while the PageRank loading scale and the loading standard
deviations for betweenness and flow share were reduced from 1 to 0.5. For the
boundary components, intercept prior standard deviations were reduced from 1.5 to
1, and boundary-loading standard deviations from 1 to 0.5. The prior centres were
retained, as were the original priors for the terminal HHI and dependence interior
components, regression coefficients, and binary-response parameters.
The third specification retained the modifications introduced in the concentrated one
and additionally introduced response-specific priors for the Beta precision
parameters.

The corresponding prior medians were set to $(\tilde{\phi}_1, \tilde{\phi}_2, \tilde{\phi}_3) = (300, 10, 10)$ for terminal
PageRank, HHI, and dependence, respectively, and to $(\tilde{\phi}_1, \tilde{\phi}_2, \tilde{\phi}_3) = (500, 100, 100)$ for
chokepoint PageRank, betweenness, and flow share, respectively. For each precision
prior, the log-scale standard deviation was fixed at 0.7.

\subsection{Prior predictive assessment}
Prior predictive simulations were conducted under the rank-one model to examine the response 
distributions implied by the three prior specifications before conditioning on the observed responses. 
The model structure was held fixed across specifications, allowing the analysis to isolate the influence 
of the prior distributions. Table~\ref{tab:prior_predictive_comparison} compares the observed standard deviations with the mean 
standard deviations across prior predictive replications under each specification.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Observed and mean prior predictive standard
deviations under the three prior specifications.}
\label{tab:prior_predictive_comparison}
\begin{tabular}{llrrrr}
\toprule
Node type & Response & Observed & ORG & CON & RS \\
\midrule
Terminal
    & PageRank & 0.0133 & 0.1065 & 0.0584 & 0.0289 \\
    & Counterparty HHI & 0.3254 & 0.2880 & 0.2935 & 0.2935 \\
    & Role-specific dependence & 0.3219 & 0.2854 & 0.2867 & 0.2867 \\
\midrule
Chokepoint
    & PageRank & 0.0069 & 0.0959 & 0.0542 & 0.0228 \\
    & Betweenness & 0.0135 & 0.0599 & 0.0329 & 0.0154 \\
    & Flow share & 0.0201 & 0.0870 & 0.0546 & 0.0278 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

For both node types, the original specification (ORG) generated substantially greater PageRank dispersion than observed. 
The concentrated specification (CON) reduced this discrepancy, while the response-specific precision specification (RS) produced further improvements. 
For chokepoints, similar reductions were observed for betweenness and flow share, with RS yielding prior predictive standard deviations closer to their empirical counterparts.

For terminals, the prior predictive standard deviations of counterparty HHI and role-specific dependence remained relatively stable across specifications and were slightly below their observed values. 
This is consistent with the unchanged response-specific precision priors for HHI and dependence between CON and RS.

However, the prior predictive distributions continued to assign excessive probability to the boundary value of one. Under RS, the mean predicted proportions were approximately 0.492 for HHI and 0.504 for dependence, compared with observed proportions of 0.341 and 0.208, respectively. 
These discrepancies remained largely unchanged across the three specifications, indicating that the alternative priors did not adequately address the excessive prior predictive boundary mass. The corresponding results are reported in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:prior_predictive_boundary}.

\subsection{Posterior sensitivity and predictive robustness}
\label{subsec:posterior_sensitivity}

To assess the sensitivity of posterior inference to the alternative prior assumptions, 
the rank-one models were re-estimated under the response-specific precision specification (RS) 
and compared with the original specification (ORG).

The alternative terminal fit produced no divergent transitions, a maximum reported $\widehat{R}$ of 1.01, a minimum bulk effective sample size (ESS) of 782, and a minimum tail ESS of 2,037. The corresponding values under ORG were 1.01, 817, and 1,958, respectively.

For chokepoints, neither specification produced divergent transitions, and the maximum reported $\widehat{R}$ was 1.01 in both cases. However, the minimum bulk ESS decreased from 472 under ORG to 359 under RS, while the minimum tail ESS decreased from 905 to 780. 
The lowest bulk ESS under both specifications was associated with the betweenness precision parameter. Under RS, this value fell below the commonly recommended minimum of 400 effective samples, indicating reduced Monte Carlo precision for this parameter.

The complete diagnostic comparison, including the parameters associated with the reported extrema, is provided in Appendix Table~\ref{tab:prior_sensitivity_diagnostics}.

Table~\ref{tab:prior_sensitivity_coefficients}
compares the posterior means and 95\% highest-density
intervals (HDIs) of the regression coefficients
under the original and response-specific prior specifications, showing that the regression coefficients remained relatively stable, with similar posterior
means and credible intervals.

For terminals, the positive associations with lagged
neighbour activity, voyage distance, capacity and
lagged exporter status were preserved, while the
intervals for terminal age and the seasonal terms
continued to include zero.
For chokepoints, the positive associations with
lagged neighbour activity and voyage distance
were also preserved, and the cosine coefficient
remained negative, with its interval just
excluding zero.

Greater sensitivity emerged for selected
response-specific parameters. In particular, the terminal loading
on the probability of HHI equal to one changed
from $-3.982$ to $-3.116$, while the chokepoint
loading on the probability of zero betweenness
changed from $-1.659$ to $-0.980$.
Both retained their negative directions, although
their magnitudes changed.

In-sample posterior predictive checks were also compared
to examine whether the alternative specification
improved the reproduction of selected observed
statistics.
Table~\ref{tab:prior_sensitivity_ppc}
reports the corresponding replicated statistics
under both specifications.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Selected posterior predictive statistics
under the original (ORG) and response-specific
precision (RS) prior specifications.
Replicated values are posterior predictive means,
except for correlations, for which medians are
reported. Checks were based on 200 replicated datasets.}
\label{tab:prior_sensitivity_ppc}

\begin{tabular}{llrrr}
\hline

\hline

& 0.290 & -0.122 & -0.121 \\

Findings show that the alternative specification produced little
change in the reported posterior predictive
statistics, leaving the main discrepancies
largely unresolved: for terminals, the replicated association between
articulation-point and incident-global-bridge status
remained negative, despite its positive observed
correlation, and the boundary frequencies of HHI and
dependence among exporters remained
inaccurately reproduced; for chokepoints, both specifications underestimated
the observed dispersion of flow share and
overestimated its median.

Given the limited changes in posterior inference and predictive performance, together 
with the minimum bulk ESS falling below the recommended threshold in the 
alternative chokepoint fit, the original specification (ORG) was retained for the main analysis. 
The response-specific precision specification (RS) therefore serves as a sensitivity assessment, 
indicating that the main posterior findings are relatively robust to the considered changes 
in prior assumptions, while the identified predictive limitations remain largely unresolved.

\section{Comparative discussion and limitations}
\subsection{Model limitations and implications}
\label{sec:limitations}

The bounded rank-one models provide an initial Bayesian
framework for examining common patterns across multiple
measures of node importance in the LNG maritime network.
Both models accommodate the bounded nature of the responses
and their observed boundary values, while the
posterior estimates satisfy the implemented MCMC diagnostic
screening criteria.

The terminal latent factor is associated with network
centrality and counterparty diversification, whereas the
chokepoint factor reflects centrality, flow exposure and
structural position. These interpretations suggest that
a shared latent dimension can summarise several aspects
of network position, although its meaning differs
between the two node types.

Nevertheless, satisfactory sampling diagnostics do not
guarantee an adequate representation of the observed
response distributions. The posterior predictive checks
identified limitations in the ability of the terminal
model to reproduce role-specific distributions and
associations between structural indicators. For
chokepoints, the main discrepancies concern the
distribution of flow share and its relationships
with other centrality measures.

The prior sensitivity analysis further showed that,
although the main regression coefficients were relatively
stable, some response-specific parameters were more
sensitive to prior choices. Moreover, the alternative
specification did not substantially improve the
posterior predictive results, highlighting that the limitations 
identified remain relevant under the considered prior
modifications.

These findings motivate several possible extensions.
For terminals, allowing response-specific intercepts
or factor loadings to vary by terminal role could help
capture differences between importers and exporters
that are not fully represented by the current model.
This distinction may be particularly relevant for
counterparty concentration: for importing terminals,
a high HHI indicates dependence on a limited number
of LNG suppliers, potentially increasing vulnerability
to supply disruptions; for exporting terminals,
by contrast, a low HHI indicates a more diversified
set of destinations, potentially reflecting broader
commercial reach within international LNG trade.
The current specification imposes a common relationship between
HHI and the latent factor across terminal roles,
potentially overlooking such differences.

Another possible extension would be to relax the
assumption of conditional independence between
responses given the latent factor. This could allow
the model to capture residual associations that
are not adequately represented by the shared
factor alone.

More flexible likelihood specifications could also
improve the representation of bounded responses,
especially where the observed dispersion or
distributional shape is not adequately reproduced,
as in the case of chokepoint flow share.

Finally, node-specific random effects could account for
persistent heterogeneity across terminals or
chokepoints and introduce dependence among
repeated observations of the same node.
A further extension would be to develop a joint
model for terminals and chokepoints, which are
currently analysed separately despite belonging
to the same maritime network. Such a framework
could allow for dependencies between the two node
types while retaining response specifications
appropriate to their different characteristics.

These extensions are not mutually exclusive, and
the posterior predictive discrepancies do not
establish which modelling assumptions are primarily
responsible for the observed shortcomings.
Their adequacy would therefore need to be evaluated
through further model comparisons and predictive
assessments.

An additional limitation concerns the estimation
sample. The models are fitted only to node-month
observations corresponding to consecutive active
months. Consequently, the latent factors describe
network position conditional on sustained observed
activity, without modelling the process determining
whether a node is active. Incorporating an activity
component would extend the analysis to both network
participation and node importance conditional on
participation.

Taken together, these limitations suggest that the
estimated latent factors should be interpreted as
preliminary summaries of network position rather
than comprehensive measures of node criticality.
The present models therefore provide a baseline
for subsequent methodological developments.

\chapter{Chokepoint geometry registry}
\label{app:chokepoint_geometries}

Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_geometry_registry} documents the geometries
used to assess route crossings for all 28 initial PortWatch
chokepoints. Published spatial geometries were used directly where
available. Geometries based on published routeing coordinates were
reconstructed as polygons. For the remaining passages, operational
corridors or crossing gates were constructed using the geographic
and navigational references indicated below. These constructed
geometries serve to classify route crossings and do not represent
official maritime boundaries. Four of the 28 passages were not
traversed under the final route-assignment specification and were
excluded from the monthly network.

\chapter{Sensitivity analysis of chokepoint assignments}
\label{app:chokepoint_sensitivity}

\section{Purpose and method}

The identification of the chokepoints traversed by each reconstructed maritime
route may be affected by small spatial discrepancies between the
\texttt{SeaRoute} paths and the operational chokepoint geometries. Such
discrepancies can arise from the spatial resolution and simplification of the
routing network, the geographic representation adopted for each passage, and
small differences between a reconstructed route centreline and the
corresponding transit geometry.

A sensitivity analysis was therefore conducted to quantify how the
route--chokepoint assignments changed under alternative spatial specifications. Four classifications were compared: exact geometric
intersection and intersections obtained after applying uniform buffers of 5,
10, and 25~km around each of the 28 candidate chokepoint geometries. The
25-km specification corresponds to the classification retained for the final
network.

For each chokepoint, distances and buffers were calculated in a local
azimuthal-equidistant projection centred on the geometry, allowing the tolerance distance to be measured in metres rather than in geographic degrees. 
Each of the 1,037 reconstructed origin--destination routes
was then compared with the original and buffered chokepoint geometries. For
every route--chokepoint pair, the analysis recorded the minimum geometric
distance, exact-intersection status, and whether the route fell within each
alternative tolerance. The same tolerance was applied to every candidate
chokepoint, thereby avoiding passage-specific adjustments based on the
observed routes.

\section{Sensitivity results}

Table~\ref{tab:chokepoint_sensitivity} reports, for each candidate
chokepoint, the number of reconstructed routes identified through exact
intersection and under the three alternative tolerance buffers. The final
column gives the number of assignments added by the 25-km specification
relative to exact intersection.

Exact intersection identified 1,809 route--chokepoint assignments involving
23 of the 28 candidate passages. Under the final 25-km specification, the
number of assignments increased to 1,964 and the number of identified
chokepoints increased to 24, resulting in 155 additional assignments, or
an 8.6\% increase relative to exact intersection.

The assignments were stable across all four specifications for 17 of the 24 chokepoints retained in the final network. 
Most of the additional assignments under the final specification were concentrated in Korea Strait and Mindoro Strait, indicating that the results for these two passages were particularly sensitive to the selected spatial tolerance.

Four candidate chokepoints---Bosporus Strait, Balabac Strait, Bering Strait,
and Kerch Strait---were not traversed under any of the evaluated
specifications: their minimum distances from the closest reconstructed routes
were approximately 71.3, 162.0, 2,617.3, and 829.7~km, respectively. They were
therefore excluded from the final monthly network. The exclusion indicates
that none of the reconstructed LNG routes in the study period passed within
25~km of their operational geometries, but it does not imply that the passages
have no relevance to maritime traffic outside the analysed LNG network.

The 25-km tolerance was retained because it accommodates spatial
misalignments between the simplified \texttt{SeaRoute} paths and the
operational transit geometries while applying a uniform and transparent
classification rule to all candidate passages. The exact-intersection
assignments were preserved separately for reproducibility, and the sensitivity
procedure did not alter the reconstructed route geometries.


\chapter{Model-ready panels variables}
\label{app:model_ready_variables}

This appendix documents the variables contained in the three model-ready panels described in Section~\ref{sec:model_ready_panels}. 
Variables are grouped according to their analytical role rather than their physical order in the CSV files. 
Flow variables are measured monthly, binary indicators take the value one when the stated condition is satisfied, and variables carrying the suffix \texttt{lag1} refer to month \(t-1\).
Log-transformed variables were calculated using \(\log(1+x)\).

The three panels cover January 2020--December 2024. Consequently, all one-month lagged variables are unavailable in January 2020. 
Measures based on historical neighbours are additionally unavailable until a node has acquired at least one neighbour through a connection observed before the current month. 
Commercial criticality measures that require realised trade are defined only for active terminal--months.
