\chapter{Full conditional posterior distributions}
\label{app:baseline_full_conditionals}

This appendix derives the full conditional posterior
distributions for the rank-one baseline model. The
derivations apply separately to terminals and chokepoints,
using the response likelihoods and original prior
specification introduced in
Sections~\ref{subsec:shared_response_likelihoods}
and~\ref{subsec:prior_distributions}.
Each full conditional is obtained from the joint posterior
in Equation~(\ref{eq:joint_posterior}) by retaining only the terms
that depend on the parameter of interest.
Multiplicative terms that are constant with respect to
that quantity are absorbed into the normalising constant.

The notation follows the main text:
$n\in\mathcal{O}$ indexes the node--month observations,
$N=|\mathcal{O}|$, and
$\mathcal{I}_j=\{n\in\mathcal{O}:0<Y_{nj}<1\}$
contains the interior observations for bounded response
$j$.
Throughout, $\textit{rest}$ denotes all remaining unknown
quantities together with the observed data and covariates.

\section{Baseline rank-one specification}
\subsection{Factor-regression coefficients}
\label{app:beta_conditional}

Let $\mathbf{X}$ be the $N\times q$ covariate design
matrix, with row $\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}$, and let
$\mathbf{f}$ collect the corresponding latent factors.

Conditional on $\mathbf{f}$, the response likelihood
does not depend on $\boldsymbol{\beta}$. Combining
the Gaussian factor-regression density with the
independent $\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^2)$ coefficient priors
therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
p(\mathbf{f}\mid\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{X})
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}).
\end{equation*}

Writing the relevant densities explicitly,
\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac12
(f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta})^2
\right\}
\right]
\left[
\prod_{r=1}^{q}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{\beta_r^2}{2(0.5)^2}
\right\}
\right].
\end{equation*}
Combining the exponential terms and using matrix
notation yields
\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})+
4\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
\Big]\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}
The first quadratic term expands as
\begin{equation*}
(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})=
\mathbf{f}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}
-2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{f}+
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}.
\end{equation*}
Removing $\mathbf{f}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}$,
which does not depend on $\boldsymbol{\beta}$, gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}+4\mathbf{I}_q)
\boldsymbol{\beta}-
2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}
\Big]\Bigg\},
\end{equation*}
where $\mathbf{I}_q$ is the $q\times q$ identity matrix.

Define
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}
=
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1},
\qquad
\mathbf{m}_{\beta}
=
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}.
\end{equation*}
Since
$\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\mathbf{m}_{\beta}
=\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}$,
completing the square gives
\begin{equation*}
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}-
2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f}=
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})-
\mathbf{m}_{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\mathbf{m}_{\beta}.
\end{equation*}
The final term is constant with respect to
$\boldsymbol{\beta}$. Thus,
\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac12
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})
\right\},
\end{equation*}
which yields the following conjugate full conditional
\begin{equation*}
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim
\mathcal{N}_q\!\left(
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{f},
\;
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\right).
\end{equation*}


\subsection{Response loadings}
\label{app:loading_conditionals}

\subsubsection{Binary-response loadings}

For binary response $k$,
\begin{equation*}
B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p_{nk}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(p_{nk})=a_k+\ell_kf_n.
\end{equation*}

Holding the factors and other response parameters
fixed, only the likelihood of binary response $k$
and the prior of $\ell_k$ depend on this loading.
Therefore,
\begin{equation*}
p(\ell_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k)
\right]
p(\ell_k).
\end{equation*}

The Bernoulli contribution is
\begin{equation*}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k)
=
p_{nk}^{B_{nk}}(1-p_{nk})^{1-B_{nk}}.
\end{equation*}
Using
\begin{equation*}
p_{nk}
=
\frac{\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)}
{1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)},
\qquad
1-p_{nk}
=
\frac{1}{1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)},
\end{equation*}
this becomes
\begin{equation*}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k)
=
\frac{
\exp\{B_{nk}(a_k+\ell_kf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior $\ell_k\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ has density
\begin{equation*}
p(\ell_k)
=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\ell_k^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}

Combining the likelihood and prior and absorbing
the Normal normalising constant of the normal prior distribution gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\ell_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{B_{nk}(a_k+\ell_kf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\ell_k^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
This expression applies to $k=1,2,3$ for terminals
and $k=1,2$ for chokepoints.


\subsubsection{Ordinary Beta-response loadings}

For an ordinary Beta response,
\begin{equation*}
Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j
\sim
\operatorname{Beta}
\bigl(\mu_{nj}\phi_j,(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j\bigr),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(\mu_{nj})
=
\alpha_j+\lambda_jf_n.
\end{equation*}
Its density is
\begin{equation*}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j)=
\frac{\Gamma(\phi_j)}
{\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
 \Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)}
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}.
\end{equation*}
The loading enters this density through $\mu_{nj}$.
Retaining the relevant likelihood and prior gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j)
\right]
p(\lambda_j).
\end{equation*}

\subsubsection{Unrestricted loading for chokepoint flow share}

For chokepoint flow share, $j=3$ and
$\lambda_3\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$, with density
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_3)=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_3^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
Substituting the Beta likelihood and Normal prior gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_3\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_3)
Y_{n3}^{\mu_{n3}\phi_3-1}
(1-Y_{n3})^{(1-\mu_{n3})\phi_3-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n3}\phi_3)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n3})\phi_3)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_3^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
Since $\phi_3$ is held fixed, $\Gamma(\phi_3)$
does not depend on $\lambda_3$ and can be absorbed
into the normalising constant. The resulting kernel is
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_3\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
Y_{n3}^{\mu_{n3}\phi_3-1}
(1-Y_{n3})^{(1-\mu_{n3})\phi_3-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n3}\phi_3)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n3})\phi_3)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_3^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}

\subsubsection{Positive PageRank loading}

For PageRank in both node types, $j=1$ and
$\lambda_1\sim\operatorname{HalfNormal}(1)$.
Its prior density is
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_1)
=
\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_1^2}{2}\right)
\mathds{1}_{\{\lambda_1>0\}}.
\end{equation*}
Combining this prior with the PageRank likelihood gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_1\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_1)
Y_{n1}^{\mu_{n1}\phi_1-1}
(1-Y_{n1})^{(1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n1}\phi_1)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_1^2}{2}\right)
\mathds{1}_{\{\lambda_1>0\}}.
\end{equation*}
Removing the factors $\Gamma(\phi_1)$, which are
constant with respect to $\lambda_1$, yields
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_1\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
Y_{n1}^{\mu_{n1}\phi_1-1}
(1-Y_{n1})^{(1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n1}\phi_1)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n1})\phi_1)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_1^2}{2}\right)
\mathds{1}_{\{\lambda_1>0\}}.
\end{equation*}

\subsubsection{Interior-mean loadings for boundary-inflated Beta responses}

\paragraph{Terminal HHI and dependence}

For terminal HHI and dependence, $j=2,3$, define
$Z_{nj}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{nj}=1\}}$. 
The boundary probability satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{logit}(\pi_{nj})
=
c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n.
\end{equation*}

Separating the boundary and interior observations,
the likelihood for response $j$ is
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
&\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,
\phi_j,c_j^T,d_j^T)
\\
&\quad=
\left[
\prod_{\substack{n\in\mathcal{O}:\\Y_{nj}=1}}
\pi_{nj}
\right]
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
(1-\pi_{nj})
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}

The probabilities $\pi_{nj}$ depend on $c_j^T$,
$d_j^T$, and $f_n$, but not on $\lambda_j$.
These contributions are therefore constant in
the full conditional of $\lambda_j$. Consequently,
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
p(\lambda_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto {}&
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
p(\lambda_j).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Substituting the $\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ prior and
absorbing the factors $\Gamma(\phi_j)$ gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_j^2}{2}\right),
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}

Thus, exact ones do not contribute to the full
conditional of the interior-mean loading when
the factors and boundary parameters are held fixed.

\paragraph{Chokepoint betweenness}

For chokepoint betweenness, define
$Z_{n2}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{n2}=0\}}$, with
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{logit}(\rho_{n2})=
c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n.
\end{equation*}
The response likelihood is
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
&\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(Y_{n2}\mid f_n,\alpha_2,\lambda_2,
\phi_2,c_2^C,d_2^C)
\\
&\quad=
\left[
\prod_{\substack{n\in\mathcal{O}:\\Y_{n2}=0}}
\rho_{n2}
\right]
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_2}
(1-\rho_{n2})
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_2)
Y_{n2}^{\mu_{n2}\phi_2-1}
(1-Y_{n2})^{(1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n2}\phi_2)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2)
}
\right].
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Since $\rho_{n2}$ does not depend on $\lambda_2$,
only the interior Beta densities and the loading
prior remain:
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
p(\lambda_2\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto {}&
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_2}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_2)
Y_{n2}^{\mu_{n2}\phi_2-1}
(1-Y_{n2})^{(1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n2}\phi_2)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2)
}
\right]
p(\lambda_2).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Using $\lambda_2\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ and removing
terms constant with respect to $\lambda_2$ yields
\begin{equation*}
p(\lambda_2\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_2}
\frac{
Y_{n2}^{\mu_{n2}\phi_2-1}
(1-Y_{n2})^{(1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{n2}\phi_2)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{n2})\phi_2)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{\lambda_2^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
As in the one-inflated case, only interior observations
contribute to this loading conditional.


\subsubsection{Loadings for the probability mass at one}

For terminal HHI and dependence, the indicators
$Z_{nj}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{nj}=1\}}$ satisfy
\begin{equation*}
Z_{nj}\mid f_n,c_j^T,d_j^T
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\pi_{nj}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(\pi_{nj})
=
c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n,
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}

When deriving the full conditional of $d_j^T$,
the interior Beta densities are constant because
their parameters do not depend on $d_j^T$.
The remaining likelihood consists of the boundary
probabilities for all observations:
\begin{equation*}
p(d_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\pi_{nj}^{Z_{nj}}
(1-\pi_{nj})^{1-Z_{nj}}
\right]
p(d_j^T).
\end{equation*}

From the logistic specification,
\begin{equation*}
\pi_{nj}
=
\frac{\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)}
{1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)},
\qquad
1-\pi_{nj}
=
\frac{1}{1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)}.
\end{equation*}
Hence,
\begin{equation*}
\pi_{nj}^{Z_{nj}}
(1-\pi_{nj})^{1-Z_{nj}}
=
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior $d_j^T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ has density
\begin{equation*}
p(d_j^T)
=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{(d_j^T)^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
Combining these terms gives
\begin{equation*}
p(d_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{(d_j^T)^2}{2}\right),
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}
Both boundary and interior observations contribute:
the former have $Z_{nj}=1$, whereas the latter
have $Z_{nj}=0$.

\subsubsection{Loading for the probability mass at zero}

For chokepoint betweenness,
$Z_{n2}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{n2}=0\}}$,  satisfies
\begin{equation*}
Z_{n2}\mid f_n,c_2^C,d_2^C
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\rho_{n2}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(\rho_{n2})
=
c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n.
\end{equation*}

The interior Beta densities do not depend on $d_2^C$.
Retaining the boundary-indicator likelihood
and the loading prior therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
p(d_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\rho_{n2}^{Z_{n2}}
(1-\rho_{n2})^{1-Z_{n2}}
\right]
p(d_2^C).
\end{equation*}
Using
\begin{equation*}
\rho_{n2}=
\frac{\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)}
{1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)},
\qquad
1-\rho_{n2}=
\frac{1}{1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)},
\end{equation*}
the likelihood contribution becomes
\begin{equation*}
\rho_{n2}^{Z_{n2}}
(1-\rho_{n2})^{1-Z_{n2}}
=
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{n2}(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior $d_2^C\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$ has density
\begin{equation*}
p(d_2^C)
=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{(d_2^C)^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
The resulting full conditional kernel is
\begin{equation*}
p(d_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{n2}(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{(d_2^C)^2}{2}\right).
\end{equation*}
Here exact zeros have $Z_{n2}=1$, whereas interior
observations have $Z_{n2}=0$, so both contribute
to inference on the zero-probability loading.

\subsection{Response intercepts}
\label{app:intercept_conditionals}

\subsubsection{Bounded-response interior intercepts}

For each bounded response, the interior intercept
enters the Beta mean through
\begin{equation*}
\operatorname{logit}(\mu_{nj})
=
\alpha_j+\lambda_jf_n.
\end{equation*}
Its prior is
$\alpha_j\sim\mathcal{N}(m_j,1.5^2)$,
where $(m_1,m_2,m_3)=(-4,0,0)$ for terminals
and $(-4,-5,-4)$ for chokepoints.

For ordinary Beta responses, all observations
contribute to the likelihood of $\alpha_j$.
For boundary-inflated responses, the boundary
probabilities do not depend on $\alpha_j$,
so only the interior Beta densities remain.
Both cases can therefore be written as
\begin{equation*}
p(\alpha_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,
0<Y_{nj}<1)
\right]
p(\alpha_j).
\end{equation*}
For ordinary Beta responses,
$\mathcal{I}_j=\mathcal{O}$.

The interior density is
\begin{equation*}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,
0<Y_{nj}<1)=
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}.
\end{equation*}
The prior density is
\begin{equation*}
p(\alpha_j)=
\frac{1}{1.5\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(\alpha_j-m_j)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Substituting these densities gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\alpha_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(\alpha_j-m_j)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Since $\phi_j$ is held fixed, the factors
$\Gamma(\phi_j)$ do not depend on $\alpha_j$.
Absorbing them into the normalising constant yields
\begin{equation*}
p(\alpha_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(\alpha_j-m_j)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\},
\qquad j=1,2,3.
\end{equation*}
The likelihood depends on $\alpha_j$ through $\mu_{nj}$.


\subsubsection{Binary-response intercepts}

For binary response $k$,
\begin{equation*}
B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(p_{nk}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(p_{nk})
=
a_k+\ell_kf_n.
\end{equation*}

Only the likelihood of response $k$ and the prior
of $a_k$ depend on this intercept. Therefore,
\begin{equation*}
p(a_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k)
\right]
p(a_k).
\end{equation*}
Using the logistic specification,
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k)
&=
p_{nk}^{B_{nk}}(1-p_{nk})^{1-B_{nk}}
\\
&=
\frac{
\exp\{B_{nk}(a_k+\ell_kf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)
}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The prior $a_k\sim\mathcal{N}(0,2^2)$ has density
\begin{equation*}
p(a_k)=
\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left(-\frac{a_k^2}{8}\right).
\end{equation*}
Combining likelihood and prior gives
\begin{equation*}
p(a_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{B_{nk}(a_k+\ell_kf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left(-\frac{a_k^2}{8}\right).
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, collecting the exponential terms
and removing those independent of $a_k$ gives
\begin{equation*}
p(a_k\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{a_k^2}{8}+
a_k\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}B_{nk}-
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\log\!\left[
1+\exp(a_k+\ell_kf_n)
\right]
\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}
This expression applies to $k=1,2,3$ for terminals
and $k=1,2$ for chokepoints.

\subsubsection{Intercepts of the probability of mass at one}

For terminal HHI and dependence, define
$Z_{nj}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{nj}=1\}}$, with
\begin{equation*}
Z_{nj}\mid f_n,c_j^T,d_j^T
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\pi_{nj}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(\pi_{nj})
=
c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n,
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}

The interior Beta densities do not depend on
$c_j^T$. Retaining the boundary-indicator
likelihood and the intercept prior gives
\begin{equation*}
p(c_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\pi_{nj}^{Z_{nj}}
(1-\pi_{nj})^{1-Z_{nj}}
\right]
p(c_j^T).
\end{equation*}

The logistic link implies
\begin{equation*}
\pi_{nj}^{Z_{nj}}
(1-\pi_{nj})^{1-Z_{nj}}
=
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior $c_j^T\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1.5^2)$
has density
\begin{equation*}
p(c_j^T)
=
\frac{1}{1.5\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(c_j^T)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Substituting gives
\begin{equation*}
p(c_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{nj}(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(c_j^T)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\},
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, removing terms independent of $c_j^T$
yields
\begin{equation*}
p(c_j^T\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{(c_j^T)^2}{2(1.5)^2}+
c_j^T\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}Z_{nj}-
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\log\!\left[
1+\exp(c_j^T+d_j^Tf_n)
\right]
\Bigg\},
\qquad j=2,3.
\end{equation*}

\subsubsection{Intercept of the probability of mass at zero}

For chokepoint betweenness, define
$Z_{n2}=\mathds{1}_{\{Y_{n2}=0\}}$, with
\begin{equation*}
Z_{n2}\mid f_n,c_2^C,d_2^C
\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}(\rho_{n2}),
\qquad
\operatorname{logit}(\rho_{n2})
=
c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n.
\end{equation*}

The interior Beta densities do not depend on $c_2^C$.
Its full conditional therefore satisfies
\begin{equation*}
p(c_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\rho_{n2}^{Z_{n2}}
(1-\rho_{n2})^{1-Z_{n2}}
\right]
p(c_2^C).
\end{equation*}

Using the logistic link,
\begin{equation*}
\rho_{n2}^{Z_{n2}}
(1-\rho_{n2})^{1-Z_{n2}}
=
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{n2}(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior $c_2^C\sim\mathcal{N}(-5,1.5^2)$
has density
\begin{equation*}
p(c_2^C)
=
\frac{1}{1.5\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(c_2^C+5)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\}.
\end{equation*}

Combining these expressions gives
\begin{equation*}
p(c_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\frac{
\exp\{Z_{n2}(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)\}
}{
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)
}
\right]
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(c_2^C+5)^2}{2(1.5)^2}
\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, removing terms independent of $c_2^C$
yields
\begin{equation*}
p(c_2^C\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac{(c_2^C+5)^2}{2(1.5)^2}+
c_2^C\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}Z_{n2}-
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\log\!\left[
1+\exp(c_2^C+d_2^Cf_n)
\right]
\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Beta precision parameters}
\label{app:precision_conditionals}

For each bounded response, the precision $\phi_j$
enters both Beta shape parameters:
\begin{equation*}
A_{nj}=\mu_{nj}\phi_j,
\qquad
D_{nj}=(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j.
\end{equation*}

For ordinary Beta responses, all observations
contribute to the likelihood of the precision parameter.
For boundary-inflated responses, the boundary
probabilities do not depend on $\phi_j$, so only
the interior observations contribute.
Retaining the relevant likelihood and prior gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\phi_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,
0<Y_{nj}<1)
\right]
p(\phi_j).
\end{equation*}

The interior Beta density is
\begin{equation*}
p(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,
0<Y_{nj}<1)=
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}.
\end{equation*}

The prior is specified on the logarithmic scale:
\begin{equation*}
\log\phi_j
\sim
\mathcal{N}(\log 10,0.7^2).
\end{equation*}
Expressed as a density for $\phi_j$, this becomes
\begin{equation*}
p(\phi_j)=
\frac{1}{0.7\,\phi_j\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(\log\phi_j-\log 10)^2}{2(0.7)^2}
\right\},
\qquad \phi_j>0.
\end{equation*}
The factor $1/\phi_j$ follows from the
change-of-variables derivative
$|d\log\phi_j/d\phi_j|=1/\phi_j$.

Substituting the likelihood and prior densities,
and absorbing constants independent of $\phi_j$,
gives the full conditional kernel
\begin{equation*}
p(\phi_j\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\left[
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{I}_j}
\frac{
\Gamma(\phi_j)
Y_{nj}^{\mu_{nj}\phi_j-1}
(1-Y_{nj})^{(1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j-1}
}{
\Gamma(\mu_{nj}\phi_j)
\Gamma((1-\mu_{nj})\phi_j)
}
\right]
\frac{1}{\phi_j}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac{(\log\phi_j-\log 10)^2}{2(0.7)^2}
\right\},
\end{equation*}
for $j=1,2,3$ and $\phi_j>0$.

\subsection{Latent factors}
\label{app:factor_conditionals}

To derive the full conditional of a particular
latent factor $f_n$, the regression coefficients,
response parameters, and all other latent factors
are held fixed. The parameter priors and the
likelihood contributions from other observations
are therefore constant with respect to $f_n$.
Retaining the factors of the joint posterior
that depend on $f_n$ gives
\begin{equation*}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
p(\mathbf{Y}_n,\mathbf{B}_n
\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})
p(f_n\mid\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{x}_n).
\end{equation*}

The Gaussian factor-regression density is
\begin{equation*}
p(f_n\mid\boldsymbol{\beta},\mathbf{x}_n)
=
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
\right)^2
\right\}.
\end{equation*}

Conditional independence of the responses gives
\begin{equation*}
p(\mathbf{Y}_n,\mathbf{B}_n
\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})=
\prod_{j=1}^{3}
g_j(Y_{nj}\mid f_n,\boldsymbol{\theta})
\prod_{k=1}^{K}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k),
\end{equation*}
where $g_j$ is the bounded-response contribution
introduced in the main text.

Denote this observation-specific likelihood by
$L_n(f_n)$. For terminals, it is
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
L_n^T(f_n)
={}&
p(Y_{n1}\mid f_n,\alpha_1,\lambda_1,\phi_1)
\\
&\times
\prod_{j=2}^{3}
p(Y_{nj}\mid
f_n,\alpha_j,\lambda_j,\phi_j,c_j^T,d_j^T)
\\
&\times
\prod_{k=1}^{3}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The bounded-response terms consist of the ordinary Beta
density for PageRank and the one-inflated Beta
contributions for HHI and dependence.

For chokepoints,
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
L_n^C(f_n)
={}&
p(Y_{n1}\mid f_n,\alpha_1,\lambda_1,\phi_1)
\\
&\times
p(Y_{n2}\mid
f_n,\alpha_2,\lambda_2,\phi_2,c_2^C,d_2^C)
\\
&\times
p(Y_{n3}\mid f_n,\alpha_3,\lambda_3,\phi_3)
\\
&\times
\prod_{k=1}^{2}
p(B_{nk}\mid f_n,a_k,\ell_k).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Here PageRank and flow share contribute ordinary
Beta densities, while betweenness contributes
the zero-inflated Beta likelihood.

Combining the observation-specific likelihood
with the Gaussian factor-regression density
and removing its constant prefactor yields
\begin{equation*}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
L_n(f_n)
\exp\!\left\{
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
\right)^2
\right\},
\end{equation*}
where $L_n=L_n^T$ for terminals and $L_n=L_n^C$
for chokepoints.

The factor enters the interior means, binary
probabilities, and boundary probabilities.
Its full conditional therefore combines information
from all responses observed for the same node--month.
Although the factor-regression density is Gaussian,
the response likelihood is a non-Gaussian function
of $f_n$, so the resulting full conditional does
not have a standard conjugate form.

\section{Random-intercept rank-one specification}
\label{app:random_intercept_conditionals}

This section derives the full conditional posterior
distributions associated with the random-intercept
extension introduced in
Section~\ref{subsec:node_specific_model}.
The response likelihoods and their parameter priors
remain unchanged from the baseline specification.
Consequently, the corresponding full conditional
kernels retain the forms derived in the preceding
sections, conditional on the latent factors.

The derivations below therefore focus on the
factor-regression coefficients, latent factors,
node-specific intercepts, and their common scale
parameter.

Let $N_{\mathrm{node}}$ denote the number of distinct
nodes included in estimation, and let
$N_{\mathrm{obs}}=|\mathcal{O}|$ denote the number
of node--month observations.
For each observation $n\in\mathcal{O}$,
let $i(n)$ identify its corresponding node.

The extended factor regression is

\begin{equation*}
f_n\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\mu_{i(n)},\mathbf{x}_n
\sim
\mathcal{N}
\left(
\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
+\mu_{i(n)},1
\right),
\end{equation*}

with hierarchical priors

\begin{equation*}
\mu_i\mid\sigma_\mu
\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_\mu^2),
\qquad
\sigma_\mu\sim\operatorname{HalfNormal}(1).
\end{equation*}

The derivations are expressed in terms of the
node-specific intercepts $\mu_i$, corresponding
to the hierarchical model formulation.

\subsection{Factor-regression coefficients}
\label{app:node_beta_conditional}

Conditional on the latent factors and node-specific
intercepts, the response likelihood does not
depend on $\boldsymbol{\beta}$.
Therefore,

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
p(\mathbf{f}\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\boldsymbol{\mu},\mathbf{X})
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}).
\end{equation*}

Combining the Gaussian factor-regression density
with the independent
$\mathcal{N}(0,0.5^2)$ coefficient priors gives

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}}
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mu_{i(n)}
\right)^2
\right]
\prod_{r=1}^{q}
\exp\left(
-\frac{\beta_r^2}{2(0.5)^2}
\right).
\end{equation*}

Let $\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}$ denote
the $N_{\mathrm{obs}}$-dimensional vector
whose $n^{nt}$ element is $\mu_{i(n)}$.
Combining the exponential terms gives

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}
-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}
-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})
+4\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}
\Big]\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}
Expanding the quadratic term,
\begin{equation*}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}
-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}
-\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta})=
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})
-2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})
+\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}\boldsymbol{\beta}.
\end{equation*}
The first term does not depend on
$\boldsymbol{\beta}$ and can be absorbed
into the normalising constant.
The resulting kernel is

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Big[
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)\boldsymbol{\beta}
-2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})
\Big]\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}

Define
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}
=
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1},
\qquad
\mathbf{m}_{\beta}
=
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}).
\end{equation*}
Since
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\mathbf{m}_{\beta}
=
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}),
\end{equation*}
completing the square gives
\begin{equation*}
\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\beta}
-
2\boldsymbol{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}})
=
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})-
\mathbf{m}_{\beta}^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}\mathbf{m}_{\beta}.
\end{equation*}
The final term is constant with respect to
$\boldsymbol{\beta}$. Therefore,

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\left[
-\frac12
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})^{\mathsf T}
\mathbf{V}_{\beta}^{-1}
(\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mathbf{m}_{\beta})
\right],
\end{equation*}
which yields

\begin{equation*}
\boldsymbol{\beta}\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim\mathcal{N}_q\Big(
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}
(\mathbf{f}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{\mathcal{O}}),
(\mathbf{X}^{\mathsf T}\mathbf{X}
+4\mathbf{I}_q)^{-1}
\Big).
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Latent factors}
\label{app:node_factor_conditional}

For each observation $n$, the full conditional
distribution of $f_n$ combines the observation-specific
response likelihood with the Gaussian factor regression:

\begin{equation*}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
L_n(f_n)
p(f_n\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\mu_{i(n)},\mathbf{x}_n).
\end{equation*}

Substituting the Gaussian density gives

\begin{equation*}
p(f_n\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
L_n(f_n)
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mu_{i(n)}
\right)^2
\right].
\end{equation*}

The likelihood contribution $L_n(f_n)$
is identical to that of the baseline model.
Consequently, the extension modifies only
the centre of the Gaussian factor-regression
term, replacing
$\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}$
with
$\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}\boldsymbol{\beta}
+\mu_{i(n)}$.

Because the response likelihood contains
Beta and Bernoulli components linked to
$f_n$ through logistic transformations,
the resulting conditional distribution
is not generally conjugate.

\subsection{Node-specific intercepts}
\label{app:node_mu_conditional}

For each node $i$, define $\mathcal{O}_i
=\{n\in\mathcal{O}:i(n)=i\},
n_i=|\mathcal{O}_i|$.
Conditional on the latent factors and
the remaining parameters, only the
factor-regression contributions associated
with node $i$ and the Gaussian prior
for $\mu_i$ depend on this parameter.
Therefore,

\begin{equation*}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
p(\mu_i\mid\sigma_\mu)
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
p(f_n\mid
\boldsymbol{\beta},\mu_i,\mathbf{x}_n).
\end{equation*}
Writing the relevant densities explicitly,

\begin{equation*}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\left(
-\frac{\mu_i^2}{2\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)
\prod_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}-\mu_i
\right)^2
\right].
\end{equation*}

Define the factor-regression residuals $r_n=
f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}$.
The conditional kernel becomes

\begin{equation*}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\left\{
-\frac12
\left[
\frac{\mu_i^2}{\sigma_\mu^2}
+
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
(r_n-\mu_i)^2
\right]
\right\}.
\end{equation*}
Expanding the sum of squared residuals,

\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
(r_n-\mu_i)^2=
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n^2-2\mu_i
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n
+n_i\mu_i^2.
\end{equation*}
Removing the term
$\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n^2$,
which does not depend on $\mu_i$, gives

\begin{equation*}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\Bigg\{-\frac12\Bigg[
\left(
n_i+\frac{1}{\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)\mu_i^2
-2\mu_i
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n
\Bigg]\Bigg\}.
\end{equation*}

Define

\begin{equation*}
V_{\mu_i}
=
\left(
n_i+\frac{1}{\sigma_\mu^2}
\right)^{-1},
\qquad
m_{\mu_i}
=
V_{\mu_i}
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n.
\end{equation*}

Since

\begin{equation*}
V_{\mu_i}^{-1}m_{\mu_i}
=
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n,
\end{equation*}
completing the square yields

\begin{equation*}
V_{\mu_i}^{-1}\mu_i^2
-2\mu_i\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}r_n=
V_{\mu_i}^{-1}
(\mu_i-m_{\mu_i})^2
-
V_{\mu_i}^{-1}m_{\mu_i}^2.
\end{equation*}
The final term does not depend on $\mu_i$.
Thus,

\begin{equation*}
p(\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
\exp\left[
-\frac{
(\mu_i-m_{\mu_i})^2
}{
2V_{\mu_i}
}
\right],
\end{equation*}
which gives the conjugate full conditional

\begin{equation*}
\mu_i\mid\mathrm{rest}
\sim
\mathcal{N}
\left(
V_{\mu_i}
\sum_{n\in\mathcal{O}_i}
(f_n-\mathbf{x}_n^{\mathsf T}
\boldsymbol{\beta}),
\;
V_{\mu_i}
\right),
\end{equation*}
where $V_{\mu_i}=
\left(
n_i+\sigma_\mu^{-2}
\right)^{-1}$.

\subsection{Node-effect scale parameter}
\label{app:node_sigma_conditional}

The full conditional distribution of
$\sigma_\mu$ depends on its Half-Normal
prior and the hierarchical Gaussian
distribution of the node-specific intercepts.
Conditional on $\boldsymbol{\mu}$,
the factor-regression likelihood does not
depend directly on $\sigma_\mu$.
Therefore,

\begin{equation*}
p(\sigma_\mu\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\sigma_\mu)
p(\sigma_\mu).
\end{equation*}

The hierarchical prior contributes

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\sigma_\mu)
\propto
\prod_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{node}}}
\frac{1}{\sigma_\mu}
\exp\left(
-\frac{\mu_i^2}{2\sigma_\mu^2}
\right),
\end{equation*}
for $\sigma_\mu>0$.
Combining the product gives

\begin{equation*}
p(\boldsymbol{\mu}\mid\sigma_\mu)
\propto
\sigma_\mu^{-N_{\mathrm{node}}}
\exp\left(
-\frac{
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{node}}}\mu_i^2
}{
2\sigma_\mu^2
}
\right).
\end{equation*}

The Half-Normal prior with unit scale has kernel

\begin{equation*}
p(\sigma_\mu)
\propto
\exp\left(
-\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{2}
\right)
\mathbb{I}(\sigma_\mu>0).
\end{equation*}

Multiplying these contributions yields

\begin{equation*}
p(\sigma_\mu\mid\mathrm{rest})
\propto 
\sigma_\mu^{-N_{\mathrm{node}}}
\exp\left[
-\frac12
\left(
\sigma_\mu^2+
\frac{
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{node}}}\mu_i^2
}{
\sigma_\mu^2
}
\right)
\right]
\mathbb{I}(\sigma_\mu>0).
\end{equation*}
Equivalently, the log-kernel is

\begin{equation*}
\log p(\sigma_\mu\mid\mathrm{rest})
=
-N_{\mathrm{node}}\log\sigma_\mu
-\frac{\sigma_\mu^2}{2}
-\frac{
\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{node}}}\mu_i^2
}{
2\sigma_\mu^2
}
+C,
\end{equation*}
where $C$ does not depend on $\sigma_\mu$.

Unlike the conditional distribution of the
node-specific intercepts, this conditional
distribution does not have a standard
conjugate form for $\sigma_\mu$.

The expressions above correspond to the
hierarchical formulation of the model.
In the computational implementation,
the node effects are represented using
the equivalent non-centred transformation
$\mu_i=\sigma_\mu u_i$, where
$u_i\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)$.
This changes the sampling coordinates,
but not the posterior distribution of
the model quantities of interest.

\chapter{Additional posterior results}
\label{app:additional_posterior_results}

\section{Baseline rank-one specification}
This section provides supplementary results for the baseline rank-one model, 
complementing the posterior analysis presented in Section~\ref{sec: rank-one model}.

\subsection{Terminals}
\label{app:terminals_add_posterior_results}

of persistent terminal-level residual patterns under

\chapter{Model diagnostics}
\label{app:model_diagnostics}

\section{Prior sensitivity analysis}
\label{app:prior_sensitivity}

\subsection{Prior predictive checks}
\label{app:prior_predictive}

Table~\ref{tab:prior_predictive_boundary} reports
the observed proportions of values equal to one
for the two one-inflated terminal responses,
together with the corresponding mean proportions
across prior predictive replications under the
three prior specifications.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\caption{Observed and mean prior predictive proportions
of values equal to one for terminal responses under
the three prior specifications.}
\label{tab:prior_predictive_boundary}
\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}
\toprule
Response & Observed & ORG & CON & RS \\
\midrule
Counterparty HHI
    & 0.3409 & 0.5063 & 0.4922 & 0.4920 \\
Role-specific dependence
    & 0.2076 & 0.4960 & 0.5042 & 0.5039 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\subsection{MCMC sampling diagnostics}
\label{app:prior_sensitivity_diagnostics}

Table~\ref{tab:prior_sensitivity_diagnostics} reports the maximum rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$ and minimum bulk and tail effective sample sizes for the original (ORG) and response-specific precision (RS) prior specifications of the rank-one models. 
All four fits were obtained using four chains, each with 1,500 warm-up iterations and 2,000 retained draws, ensuring comparable sampling configurations.

For terminals, the minimum bulk ESS was associated with the lagged exporter coefficient under both specifications, while the minimum tail ESS concerned the HHI boundary loading under ORG and a latent factor mean under RS. 
For chokepoints, the betweenness precision parameter exhibited the lowest bulk ESS under both specifications, decreasing from 472 under ORG to 359 under RS. 

The maximum reported $\widehat{R}$ was 1.01 across all four fits, indicating no substantial between-chain discrepancies according to this diagnostic. 
Nevertheless, the lower effective sample sizes for selected parameters, particularly the betweenness precision under RS, indicate reduced Monte Carlo precision for those posterior estimates.

\section{Rank-one MCMC convergence diagnostics}
\label{app:mcmc_diagnostics}

\subsection{R-hat and Effective Sample Sizes}

Table~\ref{tab:baseline_detailed_mixing}
reports the maximum rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$
and minimum bulk and tail effective sample sizes
for the baseline rank-one terminal and chokepoint models,
together with the parameters associated with
the minimum ESS values.
For terminals, the minimum bulk ESS was associated
with the lagged exporter coefficient, while the
minimum tail ESS concerned the loading governing
the probability of counterparty HHI equal to one.
For chokepoints, both minimum ESS values were
associated with the betweenness precision parameter,
indicating comparatively less efficient sampling
for this parameter.

\subsection{Geweke diagnostic}

The Geweke diagnostic compares estimates obtained
from the first 10\% and last 50\% of each chain.
The reference criterion was $|Z|\leq 1.96$,
corresponding to a two-sided significance level
of 0.05.
For terminals, 98 out of 104 parameter-chain
tests (94.2\%) satisfied this criterion, while
6 tests (5.8\%) exceeded the reference value.
For chokepoints, 73 out of 76 tests (96.1\%)
satisfied the criterion, with three exceedances
(3.9\%).
Table~\ref{tab:baseline_geweke_details}
reports the parameter-chain combinations
associated with these exceedances.

in the baseline rank-one models.}

\midrule

& Voyage distance coefficient & 2 & 2.589 \\
& HHI interior loading & 1 & -2.013 \\
& HHI interior intercept & 1 & 1.988 \\

& PageRank precision & 2 & -2.472 \\

\end{table}

\subsection{Heidelberger--Welch diagnostic}

The Heidelberger--Welch procedure assesses
stationarity and the Monte Carlo precision
of posterior mean estimates.
All 104 terminal and 76 chokepoint
parameter-chain tests satisfied the
stationarity criterion.
For terminals, 103 tests passed without
excluding any additional draws, while
one required the exclusion of 6,000
initial draws.
For chokepoints, 72 tests passed without
additional exclusions, while the remaining
four required the exclusion of between
3,000 and 15,000 draws.
Table~\ref{tab:baseline_heidel_stationarity}
reports the parameter-chain combinations
requiring additional exclusions.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\caption{Parameter-chain combinations requiring
additional initial-draw exclusions in the
Heidelberger--Welch stationarity diagnostic.}
\label{tab:baseline_heidel_stationarity}

\begin{tabular}{llrr}
\toprule
Node type & Parameter & Chain & Excluded draws \\
\midrule

\multirow{1}{*}{Terminal}
& Articulation-point binary loading & 4 & 6,000 \\

\midrule

\multirow{4}{*}{Chokepoint}
& Voyage distance coefficient & 2 & 6,000 \\
& Betweenness interior loading & 4 & 15,000 \\
& Flow-share interior loading & 4 & 15,000 \\
& Flow-share interior intercept & 1 & 3,000 \\

\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\end{table}

The half-width test assesses the precision
of posterior mean estimates by requiring
the half-width of the 95\% Monte Carlo
confidence interval to be less than 10\%
of the absolute estimated mean.
All 76 chokepoint tests satisfied this
criterion. For terminals, 100 out of 104
tests passed, while the remaining four
exceedances were associated with the
counterparty HHI interior intercept (half-width values for each chain were
respectively 0.375, 0.512, 0.578, 0.562).

\subsection{Trace plots}

The following figures present posterior densities and
trace plots for the global parameters of the baseline
terminal and chokepoint models, estimated using four
chains with 20,000 warm-up iterations and 30,000
retained draws per chain.
For both models, the posterior density estimates
of the four chains exhibit substantial overlap,
suggesting that the chains explored similar regions
of the parameter space. The corresponding trace
plots show fluctuations around relatively stable
levels, without visible systematic trends or
persistent shifts over the retained iterations.
These patterns are observed across regression
coefficients, response loadings, intercepts, and
precision parameters, including those associated
with comparatively lower effective sample sizes.
Overall, the visual diagnostics are consistent
with satisfactory mixing and support the
convergence assessment based on the
rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$ and effective
sample size statistics.

\subsection{Autocorrelation diagnostics}
\label{app:acf_diagnostics}

The following figures show within-chain autocorrelation
functions for selected global parameters from the baseline
models estimated using four chains, each with 20,000
warm-up iterations and 30,000 retained draws.
Parameters were selected using the lowest bulk ESS among
regression coefficients and response loadings, the lowest
bulk and tail ESS among all global parameters, and the
PageRank anchor loading. 
For terminals, the selected parameters are the lagged
exporter coefficient, the PageRank loading, and the
loading for the probability of HHI equal to one.
All three exhibit a progressive decline in autocorrelation,
approaching zero within approximately 30--40 lags.
The lagged exporter coefficient shows slightly more
persistent autocorrelation, while the PageRank and HHI
loadings follow similar decay patterns across chains.
The minimum bulk ESS among these parameters is 8,602,
associated with the lagged exporter coefficient.
For chokepoints, the selected parameters are the lagged
neighbour-activity coefficient, the betweenness loading
and precision, and the PageRank loading.
The lagged neighbour-activity coefficient exhibits
a rapid autocorrelation decay, reaching values close
to zero within the first few lags.
The PageRank loading also shows a relatively rapid
decline, whereas the betweenness parameters exhibit
more persistent autocorrelation, particularly the
precision parameter, whose autocorrelation approaches
zero around lag 60--70.
The latter also has the lowest bulk ESS among global
parameters (4,808).

Overall, the autocorrelation functions show consistent
decay patterns across chains, with no substantial
persistent autocorrelation at longer lags.
The slower decay observed for some parameters is
reflected in their comparatively lower, but still
substantial, effective sample sizes.

\section{Random-intercept rank-one MCMC convergence diagnostics}
\label{app:node_intercept_mcmc_diagnostics}

This subsection provides additional convergence and mixing diagnostics for the random-intercept rank-one specifications complementing the discussion in Chapter~\ref{chap:results}.
The tables report rank-normalised $\widehat{R}$ statistics and bulk and tail effective sample sizes (ESS) for selected global parameters and node-specific intercepts.
For the terminal model, the reported global parameters include those exhibiting the least favourable mixing diagnostics, together with the standard deviation 
of the node-specific intercepts. For the chokepoint model, particular attention is given to the response intercepts and node-specific effects, for which convergence difficulties were identified.

The additional diagnostics confirm substantial differences in sampling performance between the two specifications. 
For terminals, the least favourable diagnostics are associated primarily with response intercepts, while the node-specific effects exhibit comparatively satisfactory mixing.
For chokepoints, the continuous-response and several node-specific intercepts exhibit low effective sample sizes, whereas the node-effect standard deviation exhibits substantially better mixing diagnostics. 
These results indicate that the posterior summaries of individual chokepoint effects should be interpreted cautiously, as the available samples may not adequately characterise their posterior distributions.

    plot shows the PageRank continuous-response intercept.
    The four curves in each plot represent the individual MCMC chains.}
    \label{fig:terminal_node_acf}
\end{figure}
